Quasilinear wave equations on Kerr black holes in the full subextremal range | a | < M |a|<M
Mihalis Dafermos, Gustav Holzegel, Igor Rodnianski, Martin Taylor

Abstract
We prove global existence, boundedness and decay for small data solutions ψ \psi to a general class of quasilinear wave equations on Kerr black hole backgrounds in the full sub-extremal range | a | < M {|a|<M} . The method extends our previous [ DHRT22 ] , which considered such equations on a wide class of background spacetimes, including Kerr, but restricted in that case to the very slowly rotating regime | a | ≪ M |a|\ll M (which may be treated simply as a perturbation of Schwarzschild a = 0 a=0 ). To handle the general | a | < M |a|<M case, our present proof is based on two ingredients: (i) the linear inhomogeneous estimates on Kerr backgrounds proven in [ DRSR16 ] , further refined however in order to gain a derivative in elliptic frequency regimes, and (ii) the existence of appropriate physical space currents satisfying degenerate coercivity properties, but which now must be tailored to a finite number of wave packets defined by suitable frequency projection. The above ingredients can be thought of as adaptations of the two basic ingredients of [ DHRT22 ] , exploiting however the peculiarities of the Kerr geometry. The novel frequency decomposition in (ii), inspired by the boundedness arguments of [ DR11 , DRSR16 ] , is defined using only azimuthal and stationary frequencies, and serves both to separate the superradiant and non-superradiant parts of the solution and to localise trapping to small regions of spacetime. The strengthened (i), on the other hand, allows us to relax the required coercivity properties of our wave-packet dependent currents, so as in particular to accept top order errors provided that they are localised to the elliptic frequency regime. These error terms are analysed with the help of the full Carter separation.



Contents

1
Introduction

Consider the
4
-dimensional Kerr manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
, for general
subextremal
parameters
|a|<M

[
Ker63
]
.
In this paper we study quasilinear wave equations of the form

\Box_{g(\psi,x)}\psi=N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)

(1.1)

where
g(\psi,x)
and
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
are
appropriate nonlinear terms and
where

g(0,x)=g_{a,M}(x).

(1.2)

We will consider the extended exterior region of Kerr (bounded by a future spacelike
hypersurface
\mathcal{S}
slightly inside the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
) and define initial
data
(\uppsi,\uppsi^{\prime})
for (
1.1
)
on a suitable hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
which coincides with an outgoing null cone for
r\geq R
asymptoting to null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
With respect to the Kerr metric,
\Sigma_{0}
is a Cauchy hypersurface for its future in this
extended region,
which is foliated by the stationary-time translates
\Sigma(\tau)
of
\Sigma_{0}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
.
Refer to Figure
1
.

[formula]

Figure 1:
The extended Kerr spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
and the foliation
\Sigma(\tau)

The main result of the present paper is a proof of global existence, together with orbital and asymptotic stability results, for
small-data solutions of (
1.1
):

Theorem
.

For equations (
1.1
) on
the extended exterior region of an arbitrary subextremal (
|a|<M
) Kerr spacetime

(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M}(x))
satisfying (
1.2
) and under appropriate
assumptions
on the quasilinear term
g(\psi,x)
and semilinear term
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
,
we have

•

Global existence of small data solutions.
Solutions
\psi
arising from smooth
initial data
(\uppsi,\uppsi^{\prime})

on
\Sigma_{0}
, sufficiently small
as measured in a suitable
r^{p}
-weighted Sobolev energy, exist globally in
\cup_{\tau\in[0,\infty)}\Sigma(\tau)
.

•

Orbital stability.
Under the above assumptions,
the above-mentioned
r^{p}
-weighted energy flux through the time translates
\Sigma(\tau)

is uniformly bounded by a constant
times its initial value on
\Sigma_{0}
.

•

Asymptotic stability.
Under the above assumptions, a suitable lower order unweighted
energy flux through
\Sigma(\tau)
decays inverse polynomially in
\tau

(implying also pointwise inverse polynomial decay for
\psi
).

The precise statement corresponding to the above
is given as Theorem
5.1
of Section
5
.
We note already that the precise assumptions on the nonlinear terms
g(\psi,x)

and
N(\psi,x)
in our main theorem above will be exactly as in
[
DHRT22
]
(see
the discussion immediately below),
with
g(0,x)
specialised however to the Kerr metric (
1.2
).

The general framework of
[
DHRT22
]
and the
|a|\ll M
case.

We recall that our previous
[
DHRT22
]
provided stability results analogous to the above
theorem
for equations (
1.1
) under a wide class of assumptions on
g(\psi,x)
and

N(\psi,x)
. In that work,
g_{0}:=g(0,x)
is always assumed to be a background asymptotically
flat stationary metric satisfying some basic geometric restrictions (allowing in particular for the
presence of event horizons, trapped null geodesics and ergoregions) and
the semilinear term
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
is assumed to satisfy
N(0,0,x)=0
together with a generalisation of the null condition
[
Kla86
]
,
while the quasilinear term is assumed for convenience to satisfy
g(\psi,x)=g_{0}
for
x\geq R/2
for sufficiently large
R
.

Our theorem above is not included in
[
DHRT22
]
for general
|a|<M
, however, because the results of
[
DHRT22
]
depended in addition on being able to show two “black box”
assumptions on the stationary metric
g_{0}
:

1.

a quantitative boundedness and (possibly degenerate) integrated local energy decay statement
for the inhomogeneous equation
\Box_{g_{0}}\psi=F

2.

the existence of an auxiliary physical-space top order energy current for
\Box_{g_{0}}
, with bulk and boundary coercivity properties
up to specific allowed error terms.

The significance of having a physical space energy current is that simply by re-expressing it in covariant form it
can then be applied

directly
to the quasilinear wave operator
\Box_{g(\psi,x)}
, the current’s coercivity properties
immediately carrying over, up to allowed error terms. It is this fact, in conjunction with the decay provided by 1., which allows to prove top order energy boundedness estimates
for solutions of (
1.1
) without loss of derivatives and indeed
infer global existence
and stability. See already Section
1.1
for a review of the proof of
[
DHRT22
]
,
in particular also the novel consecutive spacetime slab iteration method introduced there, which allows the core of the analysis to be reduced to time-translation invariant estimates and identities above, rendering
the entire argument in the spirit of the
r^{p}
method
[
DR09
]
.

Turning to the particular Kerr case (
1.2
) considered in the present paper, the black box assumption 1. indeed holds
[
DRSR16
]
for the
full subextremal range
|a|<M
. The auxiliary identity 2., however, has only
been obtained for the very slowly rotating case
|a|\ll M
(treating it essentially as an arbitrary stationary perturbation of
Schwarzschild
a=0
), and thus, only in this regime do the
stability
results of
[
DHRT22
]
apply to the general quasilinear equation (
1.1
)
with (
1.2
).
(For the simpler
semilinear
case of (
1.1
), where

g(\psi,x)=g_{a,M}(x)

identically
, the auxiliary 2. is not in fact necessary and the results of
[
DHRT22
]

already apply for the full subextremal range
|a|<M
.)

The general subextremal case
|a|<M
: wave packet decomposition distinguishing superradiance and
localising trapping.

To handle the general quasilinear (
1.1
) with (
1.2
) for
the full subextremal Kerr range
|a|<M
, the present paper
circumvents the difficulty of showing the required
general physical space identity 2. by applying a mild
frequency decomposition on
\psi
, partitioning the solution
into finitely many “wave packets”
\psi_{n}
,
n=0,\ldots,N
, for each of which
one can obtain a suitable physical space divergence identity
with weak coercivity properties sufficient
for our argument.
This “frequency localised” version of 2. is inspired by the original energy boundedness arguments of
[
DR11
,
DRSR16
]
and by the fundamental property that superradiance and trapping are disjoint in frequency space, i.e. that wave packets localised to the superradiant regime of frequencies

0<\frac{\omega}{am}<\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}}

(1.3)

are not subject to trapping. (Here
r_{+}
denotes the Boyer–Lindquist coordinate value
of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.) For the non-superradiant wave packets on the
other hand, by making the decomposition suitably fine we can ensure that trapping
for each such wave packet
\psi_{n}
is localised to an
n
-dependent
r
-interval which
is sufficiently small to admit a timelike Killing field
\partial_{t^{*}}+\alpha_{n}\partial_{\phi^{*}}
.
Our
n
-dependent currents realising the analogue of property 2. exploit these properties
to satisfy coercivity up to allowed error terms.
Let us note already, however, that these error terms still cannot be controlled by the integrated decay statement 1. of
[
DRSR16
]
as stated there, but require a further improved linear
homogeneous estimate
which follows by the methods
of
[
DRSR16
]
but which we must also obtain in the present paper.
We give more details about the nature of the decomposition, the coercive
properties of the
n
-dependent currents and the necessary improved linear estimate in Section
1.2
below.

We emphasise that our
mild frequency decomposition
only uses the frequencies
\omega

and
m
associated to the Fourier transform
with respect to the Kerr star coordinates
t^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
, and
not
Carter’s full separation
[
Car68
]

which was used
in
[
DRSR16
]
to prove the integrated local energy statement 1. itself.
Thus, nonlinear commutation errors introduced by applying this decomposition to
\psi

are completely elementary to handle using the usual pseudodifferential
calculus.
Carter’s separation will be used however in the present paper
both to prove the necessary improved version of the estimate
of
[
DRSR16
]
and to infer the weak coercivity property
of our currents.

Background and outlook.

We refer the reader to
[
DHRT22
]
for further discussion of the stability problem for
equations of the form (
1.1
)
and the modern history of its study without symmetry, starting from the classical works
[
Kla86
,
Chr86
]
. Let us, however, explicitly point
the reader to
various previous approaches to quasilinear problems in the absence of symmetry in the Schwarzschild
a=0
and very slowly rotating
|a|\ll M
Kerr case
[
Luk13
,
LT16
,
LT20
,
LT22
,
Pas19
,
GKS22
]
(all based on previous linear work) discussed in
[
DHRT22
]
in more detail. We also refer to
[
DHRT22
]
for
comparison with the
\Lambda>0
case, but specifically point the reader
to
[
HV16
]
as well as the more recent
[
Mav21
,
Fan21
]
.
(In brief, as should be clear already from the discussion in
[
Mav21
]
,
the requisite top order estimate here arising from 2. can be shown in the
\Lambda>0
case
using Cauchy stability alone, provided that black box estimate 1. is improved to a “relatively” non-degenerate estimate of the form proven in
[
HK23b
,
Mav23
]
. Thus, the difficulties addressed in
the present paper are in fact entirely absent from the
\Lambda>0
case.)
Finally, we note that in view of
[
Civ15
]
, we expect
that the constructions of the present paper carry over essentially unchanged
to the study of (
1.1
) on
Kerr–Newman backgrounds
g_{a,M,Q}
(the charged analogue of the Kerr metric)
for general subextremal parameters
a^{2}+Q^{2}<M^{2}
.
For more on Kerr–Newman, we refer the reader to
[
Gio21
]
.
For a very recent study of the linear scalar wave equation on backgrounds settling
down to Kerr sufficiently fast, see
[
MS24
]
.

As described in
[
DHRT22
]
,
one of the motivations of the present work is to develop a setup allowing one
to
directly
promote linear stability results on fixed Kerr black hole
spacetimes, independently of how exactly these are proven,
to the statement of the nonlinear stability
of the spacetimes themselves as solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations.
In that context, our approach to the linear theory, initiated in
[
DHR
]
, centres on the extremal components
of the Newman–Penrose formalism, which decouple from the full system and
satisfy a generalisation of the linear wave equation called the Teukolsky equation
and completely parametrise the gauge invariant degrees of freedom. In our approach,
the Teukolsky equation is in turn estimated by first passing to a new
quantity satisfying the better behaved Regge–Wheeler
equation, inspired by
[
Cha75
]
.
In the nonlinear
setting (cf.
[
DHRT21
]
), the analogous quantities
satisfy “almost decoupled” wave equations which can be handled by similar techniques
to (
1.1
). Indeed, the analogue of the black box estimate
1. for the Teukolsky equation on Kerr has been
shown recently for the full subextremal range
|a|<M

in the breakthrough result
[
SRTdC20
,
SRTdC23
]
. Moreover,
not only does
[
SRTdC20
,
SRTdC23
]
provide the black box assumption 1.,
but, just as the current constructions of
[
DRSR16
]
do in the present paper, the currents
introduced in
[
SRTdC20
,
SRTdC23
]
would appear to provide precisely the key to constructing
the analogue of the necessary auxiliary
n
-dependent currents giving our new version of 2.
It is reasonable thus to expect that the characteristic “quasilinear” difficulties of the stability problem for
the Einstein equations can indeed be entirely addressed by combining our method with the results of
[
SRTdC20
,
SRTdC23
]
.
This will appear elsewhere.

To complete our understanding of quasilinear wave equations (
1.1
) on Kerr black hole exterior backgrounds, it remains only to elucidate the extremal case
|a|=M
. Here, even the linear theory is not completely understood, and it is subject to the Aretakis instability
[
Are15
]
already in axisymmetry, and to worse instabilities for higher azimuthal modes
[
Gaj23
]
.
Mode stability, on the other hand, has indeed been shown in
[
TdC20
]
.
In the simpler extremal Reissner–Nordström case, semilinear equations have been handled
in
[
AAG20
]
, under an additional structural assumption on
N
at the horizon which appears to be necessary. For conjectures concerning the nonlinear stability of extremal black
holes under the Einstein equations, see
[
DHRT21
]
(Conjecture IV.2) and
Section 1.4 of
[
KU24
]
.

Outline of the introduction.

In the rest of this introduction, we shall flesh out the above brief discussion of our proof with some more details.
We begin in Section
1.1
with a review of
[
DHRT22
]
. In Section
1.2
,
we then introduce the main new elements of our paper (the refinement of the integrated
local energy decay statement of
[
DRSR16
]
and the wave-packet localised currents which replace assumption
2. of
[
DHRT22
]
in
|a|<M
) and how to obtain from these the fundamental top-order estimate. Finally, in Section
1.3
we shall give an outline of the body of the paper.

1.1
Review of
[
DHRT22
]
: the two black-box assumptions, the fundamental estimates on
{\rm L}
-slabs and the dyadic iteration scheme

We review in this section the general scheme of
[
DHRT22
]
applied to the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
.
(The reader may wish to consult also the introduction of
[
DHRT22
]
for a more detailed overview.)

As discussed already above, the work
[
DHRT22
]
was based
on two “black box” assumptions for the linear operator
\Box_{g_{0}}
. These will be reviewed in Sections
1.1.1

and
1.1.2
below, including their extensions to higher order,

r^{p}
-weighted estimates and coercive identities. Under the assumption of appropriate null structure for
the semilinear terms
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
, this led to a hierarchy of time-translation invariant estimates,
reviewed in Section
1.1.3
,
for solutions now of the nonlinear equation (
1.1
) on a spacetime slab of
time-length
{\rm L}
, estimates which in particular
“close” both with respect to their order and with respect to their
r^{p}
weights.
Finally, this estimate hierarchy leads immediately to global existence and stability
by a pigeonhole argument and suitable iteration in consecutive spacetime slabs, briefly discussed
in Section
1.1.4
.

1.1.1
Assumption 1.: integrated local energy decay

We give here a simplified discussion of the black box assumption 1. of
[
DHRT22
]
,
specialised to Kerr (in which case the assumption was indeed proven in
[
DRSR16
]
for the
entire subextremal regime
|a|<M
).

Let us first introduce some notation for energies: Given a suitably regular function
\psi
,
we define

\mathcal{E}(\tau)[\psi]:=\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}|L\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|\psi|^{2}.

(1.4)

Here
L
and
\underline{L}
are null vector fields
in the region
r\geq R
tangent to and transverse to
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}
, respectively,
while
|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}
is comparable to the induced norm squared of the
induced gradient on spheres

\{r=c\}\cap\Sigma(\tau)
. The definition of
L
,
\underline{L}
and
|\not{\nabla}\psi|
is then extended to
r\leq R
in such a way so as
for
|L\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}
to be
a coercive expression
in first derivatives. (See already Section
2.1.3
for details.)

The expression (
1.4
)
should be thought of as the flux of energy through
\Sigma(\tau)
.
(Note that because

\Sigma(\tau)
is outgoing null when restricted to
r\geq R
, the ingoing null derivative
\underline{L}\psi

does not appear in the flux in this region,
hence the presence of the indicator function
\iota_{r\leq R}
.)

We also define the following energy quantities appearing naturally in bulk integrals:
We define the non-degenerate

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]:=\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}r^{-1-\delta}(|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2})

(1.5)

and the
degenerate

{}^{\chi}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]:=\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}\chi r^{-1-\delta}(|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}),

(1.6)

where
\chi(r)\geq 0
is a nonnegative “degeneration function” which in the Kerr case vanishes identically
in some spacetime region
\mathcal{D}
,
while
\chi=1
in
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{2}
and
r\geq R/4
, for an
r_{2}>r_{+}
. The region
\mathcal{D}

is such that all future trapped null geodesics eventually enter
\mathcal{D}
.
In particular,
\chi
vanishes at all
r
-values to which trapped null geodesics asymptote.
Finally, we define the lower-order non-degenerate quantity:

\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$-\mkern-3.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]:=\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}r^{-3-\delta}\psi^{2}.

(1.7)

The first “black box” assumption of
[
DHRT22
]
on the operator
\Box_{g_{0}}
, proved
in
[
DRSR16
]
for
g_{0}=g_{a,M}
,
was the validity of an integrated local energy estimate

\mathcal{E}(\tau_{1})[\psi]+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}(\,{}^{\chi}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]+\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$-\mkern-3.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi])d\tau^{\prime}\lesssim\mathcal{E}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}|\partial\psi\cdot\Box_{g_{0}}\psi|+|\Box_{g_{0}}\psi|^{2},

(1.8)

for arbitrary
\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}
.
The final integral on the right hand side of (
1.8
) is a spacetime integral in the region
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

bounded by
\Sigma(\tau_{0})
and
\Sigma(\tau_{1})
. In the above, we have left the
inhomogeneous term
\partial\psi\cdot\Box_{g_{0}}\psi
in schematic form (there are in fact
r
-weights associated to this, and moreover we may allow for a slightly weaker expression in the near region
r\leq R
: see already the inhomogeneous terms in Theorem
3.1
(specialised to the
case
k=0
) for the precise form of the right hand side of the inhomogeneous estimate required).

It was further shown in
[
DHRT22
]
that, given (
1.8
) and general assumptions on
g_{0}
(which obtain in the Kerr case in the full subextremal range
|a|<M
),
one may extend (
1.8
) to an estimate for a
k+1
’th order
r^{p}
-weighted
energy
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]

(defined by applying an
r^{p}
-weighted version of (
1.4
)
to
\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi
in place of
\psi
, where

\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi
is a string of commutation operators of order
k=|{\bf k}|
; see already Section
2.1.3
)

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{1})[\psi]+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}(\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi])d\tau^{\prime}\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}|\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\partial}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi\cdot\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\Box_{g_{0}}\psi|+\ldots.

(1.9)

The quantities
{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi])

represent
higher order
r^{p}
-weighted versions of (
1.6
) and (
1.5
), respectively, where
k
labels the number of commutations.
On the right hand side above, we have denoted only one of the resulting homogeneous terms—and this schematically,
with the notation
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\partial}
invoking the presence
of appropriate
r^{p}
weights!—to indicate the order
of what will turn out to be the most dangerous term later. See already Section
2.3
for the definition of all energies and
Theorem
3.1
for the precise full estimate.

For a more detailed discussion of the
r^{p}
weights appearing in the definitions, see
the introduction of
[
DHRT22
]

or see already Section
2.3
of the present paper. Here we note only
the fundamental
hierarchical relation of the
r^{p}
method between bulk and boundary energies, namely

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}{\rm\ for\ }p\geq 1+\delta,\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}{\rm\ for\ }p\geq 1.

(1.10)

It is this which allows one to extract decay in
\tau
for the lower order unweighted
energy (
1.4
) in the case of the homogeneous
wave equation
\Box_{g_{0}}\psi=0
via application of the pigeonhole principle and dyadic iteration
(see the original
[
DR09
]
).

We note already that if the estimate (
1.9
) is to be
applied directly to solutions
\psi
of the quasilinear equation (
1.1
),
we must express
\Box_{g_{0}}\psi=(\Box_{g_{0}}-\Box_{g})\psi+N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
.
One sees then that the final displayed term on the right hand side of (
1.9
)
is indeed nonlinear (thus “small”) but of higher order than
the left hand side (order

k+2
in
\psi
as opposed to order
k+1
). It follows that the estimate (
1.9
) “loses
derivatives” (see already equation (
1.25
))
and cannot be used on its own to control solutions of (
1.1
). (In contrast, in the semilinear case of (
1.1
) (where
g(\psi,x):=g_{a,M}(x)
identically), estimate (
1.9
) suffices
for global stability results,
and this is why the results of
[
DHRT22
]
already apply in that case
to the full subextremal range

|a|<M
.)
It is overcoming this
loss of derivative which motivates the second assumption, described immediately below.

1.1.2
Assumption 2.: an auxiliary top order energy current

The key to an estimate which does not lose derivatives in the quasilinear setting of
equation (
1.1
) is
to exploit covariant energy identities.
For a given metric
g
, the general covariant physical space energy identity takes the form

\nabla^{\mu}{J_{g}}_{\mu}[\psi]=K_{g}[\psi]+{\rm Re}(H_{g}[\psi]\overline{\Box_{g}\psi}),

(1.11)

where

{J_{g}}_{\mu}[\psi],\,\,K_{g}[\psi],\,\,H_{g}[\psi]

(1.12)

are energy
currents depending covariantly on the metric
g
and on the
1
-jet of
\psi
(see already Section
2.2
).

The black box assumption 2. of
[
DHRT22
]
(which we recall
was shown there for
g_{0}=g_{a,M}
in the very slowly rotating regime
|a|\ll M
but is

not
known
to hold in the general
|a|<M
case!) was the existence of a set of time-translation invariant currents (
1.12
)
satisfying (
1.11
) with
g=g_{0}

and enjoying the following coercivity assumptions:

•

The integrand
{J_{g_{0}}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}
arising in the boundary terms
on
\mathcal{S}
from integration of (
1.11
) in a spacetime domain

[formula]

Figure 2:
The region
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

(see Figure
2
)
is pointwise coercive.
The asymptotic integrand
{J_{g_{0}}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
is likewise
nonnegative.

•

The integrand
{J_{g_{0}}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}
arising in the boundary terms
on
\Sigma(\tau)
is pointwise coercive
and controls the integrand of (
1.4
).
Setting

\mathfrak{E}(\tau)[\psi]:=\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}{J_{g_{0}}}_{\mu}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)},

(1.13)

we have thus
\mathfrak{E}(\tau)[\psi]\sim\mathcal{E}(\tau)[\psi]
.

•

The bulk integrand
K_{g_{0}}[\psi]
is pointwise coercive near
\mathcal{S}
and for large
r
.
It is not necessarily globally nonnegative, however, but satisfies

-K_{g_{0}}[\psi]\lesssim\iota_{\{\chi=1\}}|\psi|^{2},

(1.14)

where
\chi
is the degeneration function appearing in assumption 1., and
\iota_{S}
denotes
the indicator function of the set
S
. We thus may write

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}K_{g_{0}}[\psi]\gtrsim\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}{}^{\rho}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]d\tau^{\prime}-C\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\iota_{\{\chi=1\}}|\psi|^{2}

(1.15)

where
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})
is defined by the expression (
1.6
)
with
\rho
replacing
\chi
,
where
0\leq\rho\leq\chi
, where again
\rho=1
for
r\leq r_{2}
, for some
r_{+}<r_{1}<r_{2}
and say
r\geq R/4
.

Let us note that the above three assumptions are in fact
stable

in the class of stationary metrics satisfying the basic geometric assumptions of
[
DHRT22
]
. Thus, they can be inferred to hold in the very slowly rotating Kerr case
|a|\ll M
as a simple corollary of
the fact that they hold in the Schwarzschild case
a=0
.

As in Section
1.1.1
,
we may now extend the above generalised coercivity statements to appropriately apply
to
k+1
’th order,
r^{p}
weighted currents

{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{J}}}_{g_{0}}[\psi],\,\,{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{K}}}_{g_{0}}[\psi],

(1.16)

which
involve summing weighted versions of (
1.12
)
applied to
\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi
over all
|{\bf k}|\leq k
.
One obtains an energy estimate

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau_{1})[\psi]+c\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]d\tau^{\prime}\leq\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+C\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}(\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi])d\tau^{\prime}+C\int|\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\partial}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi\cdot\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\Box_{g_{0}}\psi|+\ldots,

(1.17)

where again

{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]
is a higher order
r^{p}
-weighted
version of
{}^{\rho}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]
appearing in (
1.15
), and again
we have only displayed one of the arising inhomogeneous terms schematically (and we have
moreover
dropped some additional good flux terms from the left hand side).

As applied to solutions of
\Box_{g_{0}}\psi=F
, the
estimate (
1.17
) appears to be strictly worse than (
1.9
). The point, however, is that
because it arises from a covariant energy identity (
1.11
),
the identity can also be applied to solutions
\psi
of (
1.1
)
replacing

g_{0}
with
g(\psi,x)
in the definitions of (
1.12
)
.
This will generate various additional error terms
in the arising estimate analogous to (
1.17
), but
for the final displayed term of (
1.17
) we may now replace
\Box_{g}\psi
with
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
,
which is now only
k+1
’th order in
\psi
(and not
k+2
’th order),
and there is thus no loss of derivatives.
We describe this in more detail in Section
1.1.3
immediately below.

Before proceeding to the quasilinear (
1.1
),
let us remark that there is another sense in which
the estimate of (
1.17
) is better than (
1.9
), namely one has the exact constant
1

in front of the term
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]
on the right hand side. (Note
that (
1.17
) has
\leq
and not
\lesssim
.)
This is because these energies are defined as exact energy fluxes (cf. (
1.13
))
arising from identities. This fact was exploited in the scheme
of
[
DHRT22
]
to obtain the most precise results, at the expense, however, of having to always carry forward the distinction
between the quantities
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}
. Since we shall not be able to make use of
this in the present paper, we will describe a simplified scheme where we do not carry this
information further. In what follows then, we may thus apply the relation

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]\sim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi],

(1.18)

which follows from appropriate elliptic estimates, to already substitute

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)
in (
1.17
), replacing
\leq
by
\lesssim
.

1.1.3
The estimate hierarchy on
{\rm L}
-slabs for the quasilinear equation (
1.1
)

To describe the resulting estimate when (
1.11
) is applied with
g=g(\psi,x)
to solutions

\psi
of the quasilinear equation (
1.1
),
it is useful to introduce the additional “master” energies:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}+\ldots,

(1.19)

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left(\,\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime}+\cdots,

(1.20)

\displaystyle{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)d\tau+\cdots,\,\,

(1.21)

for all

\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
, where we have omitted various additional terms (including null fluxes). The
master energies (
1.20
) and (
1.21
)
combine
all the terms controlled on the left hand side of estimates (
1.9
) and (
1.17
), respectively, while the master
energy (
1.19
) satisfies

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\leq{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

(1.22)

The case
p=0
is slightly anomalous and we shall not give the formulae here. (See the introduction
of
[
DHRT22
]
or already Section
2.3.3
for the full definitions
of the master energies.)

We consider then a solution
\psi

of (
1.1
) defined on a
spacetime slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}:=\tau_{0}+{\rm L})
of time-length
{\rm L}\geq 1
satisfying an additional
lower order smallness estimate

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\lesssim\varepsilon

(1.23)

which should be viewed as a bootstrap assumption. One obtains from assumptions 1. and
2. and our basic assumptions on the
quasilinearity
g(\psi,x)
and the semilinearity
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)

the following hierarchy of estimates
for
p=2-\delta
and
p=1
, and for
\delta>0
sufficiently small:

\displaystyle{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle+\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}+\cdots\,\,

\displaystyle+{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}},

(1.24)

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle+\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}+\cdots\,\,

\displaystyle+{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}}.

(1.25)

The hierarchy extends to
p=0
in a suitable form, which is anomalous and we shall omit here.

We explain briefly the origin and significance of the various terms in (
1.24
)–(
1.25
).

Note that the left hand side of (
1.24
) is an energy with
k
commutations whereas the left
hand side of (
1.25
) is with
k-1
, i.e. is one order lower.
Equation (
1.25
) arises from
applying the linear inhomogeneous estimate (
1.9
) to
\psi
, viewing
\psi
as a solution to the inhomogeneous equation
\Box_{g_{0}}\psi=F:=(\Box_{g}-\Box_{g_{0}})\psi+N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
, whereas equation (
1.24
) arises from integrating the
divergence identity corresponding to the currents (
1.16
)
where we replace
g_{0}

with
g(\psi,x)
.

The term

{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
on the right hand side of (
1.24
)
arises precisely from the linear
term on the right hand side of (
1.17
) in view of the
definition (
1.20
) and the relation (
1.18
) (which we note
also relies on (
1.23
) in the nonlinear setting). The key to the scheme
is that this term corresponds precisely to the term on the left hand side of (
1.25
).
Thus, examining the terms on the right hand side of (
1.25
),
one may already replace this term by
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})
which in turn may be absorbed
into the term
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})
already present.

The nonlinear term
\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
on the
right hand side of (
1.24
) (together with similary omitted terms) arises from the
semilinearity
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)

in the region
r\geq R
. The fundamental point is that the appropriately measured total
r^{p}
weight in these terms
is no higher than
the
r^{p}
weight of the energy on the left hand side.
The role of the null structure
assumption (see already Assumption
4.1.1
)
on
N(\partial\psi,\psi,x)
is precisely to ensure this property.

Identical considerations apply to the term

\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
on the right
hand side of (
1.25
), which is one order lower.

Finally, the nonlinear term
{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}}
on the right hand side
of (
1.24
) and (
1.25
) arises in both cases from both quasilinear error
terms and semilinear terms in
r\leq R
(for the semilinear terms, however,
as remarked earlier,
for (
1.25
) we could here replace
k
by
k-1
in the last term on the right
hand side).
Note that in the case of (
1.24
), this term is of the same order as the left hand side,
while it is of one order more than the left hand side
in the case of (
1.25
), hence the “loss of derivatives” in (
1.25
).
Note that in this estimate we bound a spacetime integral
using the supremum term in the definition (
1.21
)
and Sobolev estimates generating
\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
, at the expense of
an estimate that depends explicitly on the length
{\rm L}
of the slab. Exploiting the presence
of the spacetime integrated terms in (
1.19
),
however, the
{\rm L}
-dependence is only
\sqrt{{\rm L}}
.

1.1.4
The pigeonhole argument and iteration in consecutive spacetime slabs

By our above comments, we may add the two estimates (
1.24
)
and (
1.25
) and obtain (under our bootstrap
assumption (
1.23
))

{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})+\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}+{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}},

(1.26)

for
p=2-\delta,1
. (Again, we omit the precise form of the inequality analogous to (
1.26
) for the
p=0
case.)
In view of (
1.22
), this allows
us to obtain global existence and estimates on an
{\rm L}
-slab under the assumption

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k^{\prime\prime}$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})\lesssim\varepsilon,\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k^{\prime}$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}^{-1}

(1.27)

for any
\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{\small$\ll$}}}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}\leq k^{\prime}-1<k^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime\prime}\leq k
. (Note we indeed need to assume estimates for some
p>0
as in the first inequality of (
1.27
) because
the anomalous
p=0
case requires an estimate on a higher
p
weighted norm in order for the estimates to close globally
in the slab.)

The above assumption (
1.27
) on its own is insufficient to obtain
existence beyond the fixed
{\rm L}
-slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
. If we embed (
1.27
), however, in a suitable hierarchy of
{\rm L}
-dependent estimates,
then not only may we propagate estimates in an
{\rm L}
-slab using (
1.26
) and its
p=0

analogue, but, we may derive—with an appeal to the pigeonhole principle in view of
the fundamental relation (
1.10
)—new
estimates at the “top”
\Sigma(\tau_{1})
of the slab which allow one to iterate the estimate in suitable consecutive
slabs, to infer
global
existence
and stability.

An example of such an “iterating” hierarchy is

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}_{i}^{\beta},\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(2-\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}_{i}^{\beta},\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}_{i}^{-1+\beta},\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu4$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}_{i}^{-1+\delta+\beta},\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu6$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{i})\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm L}_{i}^{-2+\delta+\beta},

(1.28)

where
\beta>0
is suitably small and
\tau_{i}:=\alpha^{i}
where
\alpha\gg 1
is suitably large,
{\rm L}_{i}=\tau_{i+1}-\tau_{i}

and the constants implicit
in
\lesssim
are chosen accordingly.

This yields the global existence statement of the main theorem
and the asymptotic stability statement, for instance the decay

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu6$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)\lesssim\varepsilon\tau^{-2+\delta+\beta}

(1.29)

for the lower order unweighted energy.

Let us note that the above hierarchy (
1.28
) upon iteration appears to yield

\tau^{\beta}
growth for the highest order norm
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)
. Revisiting
however (
1.26
) in the global (non-dyadic!) slab
\mathcal{R}(1,\tau)
, one may estimate the nonlinear
terms on the right hand side by summing the estimates on the dyadic subintervals already obtained
and thus obtain the uniform boundedness statement

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)\lesssim\varepsilon.

(1.30)

Thus we obtain also the top order orbital stability statement of the main theorem.

Note that the iterating hierarchy (
1.28
) presented in this section
was not the one actually
used in Section 6.3.4 of
[
DHRT22
]

but is related to that
of Section 6.2.5 of that paper (which was there used however only in the context of the restricted case (ii) considered in that section).
In contrast, the original hierarchy of Section 6.3.4 of
[
DHRT22
]

exploited the distinction between
the two energies (
1.18
) to obtain slightly more precise results, in particular
to obtain the uniform boundedness (
1.30
)
without having to revisit the estimates in the global slab
\mathcal{R}(1,\tau)
.
As we shall not be able to do this
in the present paper, we have described in this section
exactly the simplified scheme which we shall actually
use for the general
|a|<M
case. (See already Section
6
.)

1.2
The wave-packet localised coercive currents and the top order energy estimate
for
|a|<M

We describe in this section in more detail the main new ideas of the present paper.

We first review in Section
1.2.1
the basic frequency analysis
associated to
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
on Kerr as used in
[
DRSR16
]
, distinguishing
the
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
analysis (for which we will introduce here a rudimentary pseudo-differential
calculus) from Carter’s full separation.
We then proceed in Section
1.2.2
with a discussion of
an improvement to the local integrated decay estimate
(assumption 1. of
[
DHRT22
]
reviewed
in Section
1.1.1
) which
can easily be shown from
[
DRSR16
]
for the full subextremal range
|a|<M
of Kerr and will play
an important role in our argument. With this, we shall describe our wave-packet localised constructions in
Section
1.2.3
, replacing
the role of 2. above. We will then describe in Section
1.2.4
how in conjunction with the improved integrated decay estimate strengthening 1., these result in a suitable top order estimate. Finally, we will briefly remark how to complete the proof in Section
1.2.5
.

1.2.1
Frequency analysis of
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
: The
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
frequency analysis and pseudo-differential calculus and Carter’s full separation

The Killing symmetries
\partial_{t^{*}}
and
\partial_{\phi^{*}}
of the Kerr metric (here

(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)
denote Kerr star coordinates—see already Section
2.1.1
)
allow
us to define frequency projections via the Fourier transform:

\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\omega t^{*}}e^{-im\phi^{*}}\Psi(r,\theta,t^{*},\phi^{*})dt^{*}d\phi^{*},

(1.31)

where
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It follows then that given a suitable smooth function
0\leq\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}(\omega,m)\leq 1
, the
“projection” operator

P=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}\,\mathfrak{F}),

(1.32)

defined on an appropriate functional domain,
commutes with
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
. Under the usual decay assumptions on
\omega
-derivatives
and
m
-differences of
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}(\omega,m)
, operators of the form (
1.32
) will be the simplest examples
of what we will term
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
pseudodifferential operators
. Versions of
the standard pseudodifferential calculus apply for operators of this type and operators that
arise by commuting (
1.32
) with suitable spacetime differential operators.
See already Section
3.2.2
. In practice, this will allow us
to commute
\Box_{g(\psi,x)}
with appropriate zeroth order pseudodifferential projection operators like (
1.32
)
above and estimate without loss of derivatives, in an entirely analogous
way as commutation with usual differential operators.

The Fourier transform (
1.31
) already allows us to define the superradiant frequencies

(\omega,m)
to be those
satisfying (
1.3
).
(Note that such frequencies exist only when
a\neq 0
. For convenience, let us assume
a\neq 0
in what follows.)
All other frequencies
(\omega,m)
will be known as
nonsuperradiant
. Let us note that if
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}
in (
1.38
) is supported
in the nonsuperradiant frequencies, then

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}J^{T}[P\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\geq 0

(1.33)

where
J^{T}_{\mu}
denotes the standard energy current corresponding to
the stationary Killing vector field
T=\partial_{t^{*}}
.
(The separation of the superradiant and nonsuperradiant part of the solution
via projections as above to ensure in particular (
1.33
) was already exploited
in
[
DR11
]
.)

Let us note already that another source of error terms which will arise
in this paper from applying pseudodifferential operators concern when we try to localise
integrated coercivity and nonnegativity statements like (
1.33
) to smaller domains.
These error terms will be “linear” but will typically—but not always!—be lower order.

On top of the Fourier transform (
1.31
), we shall also use
Carter’s full separation
[
Car68
]

to analyse functions
\Psi
, i.e. we will apply to
U=\mathfrak{F}[\Psi]

the further transformation

\mathfrak{C}[U](r,\ell,\omega,m)=\int_{0}^{\pi}U(r,\theta,\omega,m)\,\overline{S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}}(\theta)\,d\theta.

(1.34)

Here
S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}

label the so-called oblate spheroidal harmonics. A rescaled and adjusted version of

\mathfrak{C}[U]
, denoted
u(r,\ell,\omega,m)
,
satisfies an ordinary differential equation

u^{\prime\prime}+\omega^{2}u-V(\omega,m,\ell)u=G

(1.35)

where the inhomogeneity
G
arises from
\Box_{g_{a,M}}\Psi
and prime-notated
differentiation
′
is to be understood with respect
to a rescaled version of
r
denoted
r^{*}
, which satisfies
r^{*}\to-\infty
as
r\to r_{+}
. We shall only apply (
1.34
)
in some region
r_{+}<r\leq R_{\rm freq}<R

outside the Kerr event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

The additional frequency parameter
\ell
allows one to localise the
trapping phenomenon to the frequency range
\omega^{2}\sim\Lambda(\omega,m,\ell)\gg 1
, where
\Lambda
is related to the eigenvalues associated to the oblate spheroidal harmonics
S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}
.
The proof of (
1.9
)
in
[
DRSR16
]
was based on fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)
-frequency
energy currents which in particular capture the unstable property of trapping. This in turn
is related to the fact that the potential
V
in (
1.35
)
has a unique non-degenerate maximum
\hat{V}_{\rm max}
which
in the true trapping frequency range satisfies
\omega^{2}\approx\hat{V}_{\rm max}
.
See already Section
A.2
for a review of these currents in the
formalism of
[
DRSR16
]

and their relation with physical space currents (
1.12
).

As noted at the beginning of this paper, we emphasise that we shall not attempt to define pseudodifferential operators using projections defined by (
1.34
) or commute such operators with

\Box_{g(\psi,x)}
. The role of Carter’s separation (
1.34
) will be to revisit
[
DRSR16
]
in order to prove a stronger
version of (
1.9
) (see Section
1.2.2
immediately below) and to infer coercivity of our currents modulo error terms precisely
related to this stronger estimate (see already Section
1.2.3
).

1.2.2
An improved integrated local decay estimate

Before discussing the replacement of assumption 2. of
[
DHRT22
]
, let us
describe how a stronger version of the integrated local decay assumption (
1.8
)
may be obtained in the Kerr case in the full subextremal regime
|a|<M
. For details, see already Section
3.3
.

We first note that in
[
DRSR16
]
,
a stronger estimate than (
1.8
) is in fact already shown,
which can only however be expressed in terms of the frequency
analysis with respect to Carter’s full separation reviewed in Section
1.2.1
.
In particular, restricted to superradiant
frequencies (
1.3
), the bulk integral does not in fact degenerate,
whereas, for the non-superradiant frequencies, the degeneration may be localised
to an
(\omega,m,\ell)
-frequency dependent fixed
r
-value related to the maximum

\hat{V}_{\rm max}

of the potential
V
in (
1.35
) when this maximum moreover satisfies

\hat{V}_{\rm max}\approx\omega^{2}
.
See already
Section
3.3.1
.

What is more however, by exploiting appropriate fixed-
(\omega,m,\ell)
currents for (
1.35
), it turns out that the frequency localised estimate of
[
DRSR16
]
can further be improved
in the frequency range

V-\omega^{2}\gtrsim b_{\rm elliptic}\omega^{2}

(1.36)

in order to gain suitable powers of
\omega
and

m
, rendering the estimate effectively elliptic.
Thus, after commutation, we in fact control a stronger quantity than
{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
in (
1.25
), a quantity we shall here
denote as

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

(1.37)

which in fact contains one order more control of
\psi
restricted to certain

(\omega,m,\ell)
-frequencies and
regions of spacetime (hence, the

[formula]

). See already Sections
3.3.2
–
3.3.3
.

Controlling (
1.37
) will allow us
to in fact accept certain
top order
errors in our coercivity, provided that these are localised in
both physical and Carter frequency space, to this “elliptic”
region.
We will see this in practice in Section
1.2.3
below (see
already (
1.45
)).

1.2.3
The frequency projections
P_{n}
, wave-packet localised currents and their coercivity

With the above preliminaries, we are ready to describe in more detail the novel replacement of
assumption 2. of
[
DHRT22
]

and how it leads to a weaker version of (
1.17
)—still sufficient for
our purposes however!

Fix a spacetime slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and let

\psi
be a smooth function defined on that slab.

We first introduce the frequency projections which will allow
us to partition
\psi
into wave packets.

We
define
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}(\omega,m)

to be appropriate smooth functions,
n=0,\ldots,N
,
which will make the projection

P_{n}=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}\,\mathfrak{F})

(1.38)

into a zeroth order
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator,
and decompose
\psi
into wavepackets

\psi_{n}:=P_{n}(\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi)

(1.39)

(defined with the help of a spacetime cutoff function
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}
which localises

\psi
to a spacetime slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
).

As mentioned already,
our decomposition into wave packets
\psi_{n}
given by (
1.39
)
will in particular separate out the non-superradiant
part of
\psi
from the superradiant part supported on frequencies (
1.3
). It will further
localise the original
\psi
, however, in finer frequency ranges based on the value of
\omega/am
.
Thus we will have in fact a set
\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{N_{s}}
of superradiant wave
packets and a remaining set
\psi_{N_{s}+1},\ldots,\psi_{N}
of nonsuperradiant wave packets.
(See already Sections
3.4.1
and
A.1.5
.)
The fundamental goal of the decomposition into (
1.39
) is the following:

1.

For non-superradiant packets
\psi_{n}
, by requiring that the decomposition
be sufficiently fine,
one can ensure that trapping is localised in physical space
to an (arbitrary) small
r
-range
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, depending only on
n
,
on which there is a fixed timelike Killing field

T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}

(1.40)

in the span of
T=\partial_{t}
and
\Omega_{1}=\partial_{\phi}
. We note that such a vector field
and a related decomposition already played a role in showing the boundedness
part of statement (
1.8
)
in Section 13 of
[
DRSR16
]
.

2.

For superradiant packets
\psi_{n}
, on the other hand, which by the above remarks are not subject to trapping, again
by requiring that the decomposition be sufficiently fine, one can ensure that there is an

r
-range depending only on
n
where the wave operator
\Box_{g_{a,M}}

is effectively elliptic and the derivative improvement
of Section
1.2.2
applies.

Property 1. above is to be compared with the discussion of
Section
1.2.2
, where we note that for fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)
, the degeneration associated to trapping is associated to a unique
r(\omega,m,\ell)
related
to the maximum
V_{\rm max}(\omega,m,\ell)
. As remarked in Section
1.2.2
above, however,
true trapping requires also this maximum
to satisfy
V_{\rm max}(\omega,m,\ell)\approx\omega^{2}
and this only happens at an
r
-value which is uniquely
determined by (and depends continuously on)
\omega/am
. See already
Section
A.1.4
.

Property 2. above is in fact related to the original proof of
(\omega,m,\ell)
-localised
energy estimates for superradiant frequencies in
[
DRSR16
]
, where for fixed
\ell
,
the existence of an
r
-interval satisfying (
1.36
) was exploited.
The fact that one may choose such an interval depending only on
\omega/am
follows
from the monotonicity properties of
V
in
\ell
. The existence then of a suitable interval

depending now only on
n
follows by continuity for a sufficiently fine decomposition. See already
Section
A.1.3
.

The above properties of the decomposition allow for the construction of
n
-dependent currents

{J^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{0}}},\qquad K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{0}}

(1.41)

related by (
1.11
), which in turn have the following coercivity properties:

•

In the region
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
for large enough
R_{\rm freq}\leq R/4
, the currents are
n
-independent and have
the good boundary and bulk coercivity properties identical to those of their analogues in
[
DHRT22
]
described in Section
1.1.2
above.

•

For all
n
,
the boundary terms
\int{J_{g_{0}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi_{n}]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}
, integrated
on general subsets
\mathcal{S}(\bar{\tau}_{1},\bar{\tau}_{2})\subset\mathcal{S}
, are nonnegative
up to allowed errors arising from pseudodifferential localisation. See already
Proposition
3.4.9
.
For superradiant
n
, we in fact have the pointwise coercivity

{J_{g_{0}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq c(|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2})-C|\psi|^{2}\qquad\text{\ on\ }\mathcal{S},

(1.42)

(see already Proposition
3.4.6
).

•

For non-superradiant frequency ranges,
\int K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{0}}[\psi_{n}]

is coercive after integration in spacetime
in the region
where

{J_{g_{0}}^{{\rm main},n}}\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}

fails to be coercive
,
up to lower order terms, but also top order terms in the elliptic frequency range discussed in Section
1.2.2
, and errors
arising from pseudodifferential localisation.
More precisely, we may define for each
n
a sufficiently regular
degeneration function
\chi_{n}(r)
, vanishing identically in the region
\mathcal{D}_{n}

discussed above,
and with

\chi_{n}=1\text{\ on\ }\mathcal{M}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n},

(1.43)

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\supset\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a slightly larger region,
such that

\displaystyle\int K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{0}}[\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}]

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle\int\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left(|L\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}\right)

(1.44)

\displaystyle\qquad-C\,\,{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

(1.45)

\displaystyle\qquad-\ldots

where integration is over

\mathcal{R}(\bar{\tau}_{1},\bar{\tau}_{2})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}
,
where
\mathcal{R}(\bar{\tau}_{1},\bar{\tau}_{2})\subset\mathcal{R}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is
a general subregion, and where
\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}
is a string of commutation
operators involving only
T
and
\Omega_{1}
(and thus commutes with
P_{n}
).
Inequality (
1.44
)
should be compared with (
1.15
) (after commutation with

\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}
). Note the presence of the
stronger quantity (
1.37
) on line (
1.45
) which
now includes also top order terms.
The omitted error terms arise
from pseudodifferential localisation.

On the region
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
on the other
hand, we have pointwise coercivity of the boundary current, i.e.

{J_{g_{0}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}\gtrsim|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+|\psi|^{2}\text{\rm\ on\ }\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cap\Sigma(\tau).

(1.46)

•

For superradiant frequency ranges,
\int K_{g_{0}}^{{\rm main},n}[\psi_{n}]
is coercive
after integration in spacetime
without degeneration
, up to lower order terms
and top order terms in the elliptic frequency range, and errors arising from
pseudodifferential localisation. That is to say, we have (
1.44
)
without the presence
of the degeneration function
\chi_{n}
. For consistency, it will be convenient
to set
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\emptyset
and define
\chi_{n}:=1
identically.
Then (
1.43
) and (
1.44
) hold as stated for both superradiant and nonnsuperradiant
frequency ranges
n
.

To prove our bulk and boundary coercivity
properties (
1.44
), (
1.46
) and (
1.42
)
we build our current from
various components (see already Section
3.4.2
).

The most delicate component of the current ensuring the bulk coercivity (
1.44
)
will be a current of the form
\tilde{J}^{y_{n}}
associated to a vector field
2y_{n}(r)\partial_{r^{*}}

(see already (
A.67
)), where
y_{n}(r)
is a non-decreasing function
of
r
which
vanishes identically
in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
The associated bulk current
\tilde{K}^{y_{n}}
then also vanishes identically in

\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
For superradiant ranges
n
, in which case
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\emptyset
,
we choose
y_{n}
to simply change sign at some value
of
r
in the elliptic region provided by 2.

It is in order to show
nonnegativity of
\tilde{K}^{y_{n}}
modulo our “allowed” error terms
that
we must use
Carter’s separation and the current formalism of
[
DRSR16
]
.
This is because the allowed error term (
1.45
) itself is characterised
by the elliptic improvement discussed in Section
1.2.2
,
which in turn depends on
Carter’s separation.
To show then the requisite nonnegativity, we
frequency decompose
\tilde{K}^{y_{n}}[\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi]

and examine its action on fixed frequencies
(\omega,m,\ell)
.
The required coercivity can then be reduced to a fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)
-frequency coercivity statement with error terms which are allowed to be
(\omega,m,\ell)
-dependent but
must be controlled by (
1.45
).
In practice, this means that at the fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)
level,
one can even accept top order failure of positivity in the
region (
1.36
),
as this corresponds precisely
to the elliptic region discussed in Section
1.2.2
,
and thus suitable control is indeed
provided by (
1.45
).
It thus suffices to design
y_{n}
so that the fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)
-frequency
bulk has nonnegativity properties outside of (
1.36
).
It is precisely the properties of the potential
V
discussed above
which ensure that this is indeed possible.
See already Propositions
A.4.3
and
A.4.5
.

The
boundary coercivity property (
1.46
)
on
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

for nonsuperradiant frequency regimes
is ensured by adding a large multiple times the current

J^{T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}}

(1.47)

associated to (
1.40
), which we recall is timelike in
\mathcal{D}_{n}

(and thus also in some
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\supset\mathcal{D}_{n}
), suitably cut off for large
r
.
The boundary coercivity property (
1.42
) on the other hand for superradiant
frequency regimes is ensured by
adding a large multiple of (
1.47
) with the choice

\alpha_{n}:=\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}
in which case
T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}
coincides with the Hawking
vector field (
2.3
) which is future directed, null on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Requiring
now
r_{0}
to be sufficiently near
r_{+}
, and adding a small multiple times the
redshift current of
[
DR13
]
, one indeed ensures (
1.42
).
Adding the redshift current also ensures non-degeneracy of bulk terms near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Finally, the integrated boundary coercivity on
\mathcal{S}

property valid for non superradiant frequencies essentially
follows from the analogue of (
1.33
) applied to the current (
1.47
).
See already Section
A.5
.

The remaining coercivity properties referred to above and the
n
-independence of the currents
in the region
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
are ensured
by adding additional well-chosen current components. As mentioned above, one must cut off
the component (
1.47
)
so as to coincide with an
n
-independent multiple of
J^{T}
for large
r
. This is somewhat delicate
in the case of non-superradiant
n
(see already (
A.104
)).
For superradiant
n
on the other hand, the cutoff can be chosen to be supported in the
r
-range (depending only on
n
) for which the
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
is elliptic (recall property 2. above),
and thus all error terms generated by the cutoff can be easily absorbed by (
1.45
).

1.2.4
The new top order energy estimate on
{\rm L}
-slabs

We finally turn to the quasilinear equation (
1.1
). As before,
we fix a slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and suppose
we have a smooth solution
\psi
of (
1.1
) defined
on the slab. We define the wave packets
\psi_{n}
by (
1.39
) as before.

We now define energies
{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bdry}
and
{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}

corresponding to quantities which are indeed controlled coercively
by (
1.46
) and (
1.44
) in
the boundary
and bulk respectively,
summed over wave packets:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}{}_{{\rm bdry}}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n}\sum_{|{\bf k}|=k}\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cap\Sigma(\tau)}|L\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+r^{p}\text{-weighted terms in\ }\{r\geq R_{\rm freq}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\text{improved quantities near $\mathcal{S}$},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}{}_{{\rm bulk}}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n}\sum_{|{\bf k}|=k}\int_{(\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\cap\Sigma(\tau)}|L\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+r^{p}\text{-weighted terms in\ }\{r\geq R_{\rm freq}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\text{improved quantities near $\mathcal{S}$},

where we recall the convention
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\emptyset
for superradiant ranges
n
.
The additional terms added on the right hand side are
n
-independent.
See already Section
4.3.3
for precise definitions.

Defining

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau):=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}{}_{{\rm bdry}}(\tau)+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}{}_{{\rm bulk}}(\tau)

then using appropriate elliptic estimates (see already Section
2.3.5
)
and in view also of the extra terms added in the second
and third lines of the above definitions, we have

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau)+\ldots,\qquad{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}{}_{{\rm bulk}}(\tau)+\ldots,

(1.48)

while, using also the properties of the pseudodifferential calculus and local existence, we have

\int_{\tau}^{\tau+1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)+\ldots,

(1.49)

where the omitted terms are all error terms which are “allowed”. (See already Proposition
4.3.5
.)

Integrating in a general subregion

\mathcal{R}(\bar{\tau}_{0},\bar{\tau}_{1})\subset\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

the divergence identities of the currents (
1.41
) applied to
\psi_{n}
,
where we covariantly now replace
g_{0}
with
g(\psi,x)
in their definitions,
and summing over
n
and
|{\bf k}|=k
we obtain

\displaystyle{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bdry}(\bar{\tau}_{1})+\int_{\bar{\tau}_{0}}^{\bar{\tau}_{1}}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\tau)d\tau\lesssim{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bdry}(\bar{\tau}_{0})+{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\bar{\tau}_{0})+{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\bar{\tau}_{1})

(1.50)

\displaystyle+{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

(1.51)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}+\cdots+{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}}

(1.52)

\displaystyle+\cdots.

(In obtaining the above, we have also separately added in an
n
-independent divergence identity in the far away region
and another such identity close to
\mathcal{S}
.)
We note the term
{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
on line (
1.51
)
which arises from (
1.45
),
and the nonlinear terms (
1.52
) which arise
as before. There are additional error terms, linear and nonlinear, arising
from pseudodifferential commutation, but these can finally be absorbed in the other
errors terms or finally in the main terms estimated. We shall not discuss the omitted
terms further here. See already (
4.45
)–(
4.56
) for the full
estimate.

At first glance, the presence of the future boundary term

{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\bar{\tau}_{1})

(1.53)

on the right hand
side may
seem an obstacle to infer boundedness estimates at top order from (
1.50
).
It turns out that given a solution
\psi
of (
1.1
) existing globally in a slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, the inequality (
1.50
) would be sufficient to obtain quantitative top order boundedness
if only we had some a priori nonquantitative control of (
1.53
) for all times
\bar{\tau}_{1}

and we could apply the inequality in regions

\mathcal{R}(\bar{\tau}_{0},\bar{\tau}_{1})
where
\bar{\tau}_{1}
is possibly far in the future of

\tau_{1}
. While at this point of the argument
we do not even know that
\psi
exists in the future of

\Sigma(\tau_{1})
, let alone that it be
well behaved to the future, we may, by cutting off the nonlinearities near
\Sigma(\tau_{1})
(instead of cutting off
\psi
) and then re-solving the equation, extend
\psi
to a
\widehat{\psi}
which for later
\tau\geq\tau_{1}
is a solution to the linear equation:

\Box_{g_{a,M}}\widehat{\psi}=0,\qquad\tau\geq\tau_{1}.

(1.54)

See already Section
4.3.2
.
All the above estimates and relations
in fact then apply with
\widehat{\psi}
in place of
\psi
.
In view of the uniform boundedness estimate for solutions
of (
1.54
) (which follows from (
1.9
)),
we have the nonquantitative statement:

\lim_{\bar{\tau}_{1}\to\infty}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\bar{\tau}_{1})[\widehat{\psi}]<\infty.

(1.55)

It follows that applying (
1.50
) with a large enough
\bar{\tau}_{1}
, we may obtain a quantitative estimate on

{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathfrak{E}}}}_{\rm bulk}(\tau_{\rm late})[\widehat{\psi}]
for some
\tau_{\rm late}\geq\tau_{1}
by
applying the pigeonhole principle to the bulk integral on the left hand side.
We may now plug this estimate back into (
1.50
)
to remove any dependence on a future boundary
term (
1.53
). See already Lemma
4.5.3
.

In view of the relations (
1.48
)–(
1.49
)
and the above comments, we finally obtain from (
1.50
)
the estimate

{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})+{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}+\cdots+{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\sqrt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sqrt{{\rm L}},

(1.56)

which we recognise to be (
1.24
)
with
{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
in
place of
{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

1.2.5
Finishing the proof

In view of the fact that by our comments in Section
1.2.2
,
we have that a stronger version of (
1.25
) holds with

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
replacing

{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
on the left hand side, then this together
with (
1.56
) may again be combined and used essentially
unchanged in the scheme outlined
previously in Section
1.1.4
involving the pigeonhole principle and iteration
in consecutive spacetime slabs.
We obtain in particular global existence, the top order boundedness
statement (
1.30
)
and the lower order decay (
1.29
).
See already Section
6

for the details.
This will then complete the proof.

1.3
Outline of the paper

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows:
In Section
2
, we shall review the Kerr manifold and
basic notations from
[
DHRT22
]
. We shall then state in Section
3

the black box assumption 1. as proven already in
[
DRSR16
]
(and deduce the refinement discussed
already in Section
1.2.2
above), and introduce the new set of weakly coercive currents applied to projections discussed above in Section
1.2.3
, which will replace assumption 2. We will turn to the study of the quasilinear
equation (
1.1
) itself
in Section
4
and deduce in particular the main top order estimate
discussed above in Section
1.2.4
.
We give a precise statement of the main theorem in Section
5

and
carry out its proof in Section
6
, using the main estimate above
and the scheme outlined in Section
1.1.4
. All arguments making use of
Carter’s separation are deferred to Appendix
A
, which
