reviews in detail the fixed frequency current formalism of
[
DRSR16
]
.

The reader will notice that we have followed closely the structure of
[
DHRT22
]
wherever
possible so as to facilitate comparison.

MT acknowledges support through Royal Society Tata University Research Fellowship URF\R1\191409.

2
The subextremal Kerr manifold and basic notations

In this section we review
the Kerr manifold and the framework of
[
DHRT22
]
, recalling in particular
all notations to be used in the paper.

We begin in Section
2.1
reviewing how Kerr is put in the setting of our general
assumptions of
[
DHRT22
]
.
We shall review the formalism of covariant energy identities for wave operators in
Section
2.2

and finally, review our basic energy notations from
[
DHRT22
]
in Section
2.3
.

2.1
The subextremal Kerr manifold in the framework of
[
DHRT22
]

We have already described in
[
DHRT22
]
(see Section 2.7.3 and Appendices A and C.2
therein) how
the Kerr metric fits into the framework of that paper for the full subextremal range
|a|<M
.
We review briefly here.

2.1.1
Boyer–Lindquist and Kerr star coordinates, differentiable structure and the function
r

Fixing subextremal parameters
|a|<M
, the Kerr metric is given in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t,r_{BL},\theta,\phi)
by the expression:

\displaystyle g_{a,M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta}{r_{BL}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta d\phi)^{2}+\Delta^{-1}(r_{BL}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)dr_{BL}^{2}

(2.1)

\displaystyle\qquad+(r_{BL}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta(r^{2}_{BL}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)^{-1}(adt-(r_{BL}^{2}+a^{2})d\phi)^{2},

where

\Delta:=r_{BL}^{2}-2Mar+a^{2}=(r_{BL}-r_{-})(r_{BL}-r_{+}),\qquad r_{\pm}:=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}.

The metric expression (
2.1
)
extends to be defined on a larger region which can be put in the form described in Section 2.7.3 of
[
DHRT22
]
.

The extension can be defined with the help of so-called Kerr star coordinates

(r_{BL},t^{*},\phi^{*},\theta)
which in
r_{+}>r_{BL}
are related to Boyer–Lindquist
coordinates by the transformations

t^{*}=t+\bar{t}(r_{BL}),\qquad\phi^{*}=\phi+\bar{\phi}(r_{BL})\mod 2\pi,

where for the explicit form of the functions

\bar{t}
,
\bar{\phi}
, see
[
DRSR16
]
. (These functions may be chosen
so as to be supported in
r_{BL}\leq 9M/4
for instance.)
Given
r_{0}
with
r_{-}<r_{0}<r_{+}
, the expression would define a smooth metric on the ambient
manifold-with-boundary

\mathbb{R}^{4}_{(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})}\setminus\{r_{BL}<r_{0}\}
where
r_{BL}
is identified with the
usual radial function of the spatial Cartesian coordinates,
and
t^{*}
is identified with
x^{0}
. We do a slightly different
identification, that is, given
r_{0}
as above and
R
sufficiently large, we define the metric on

\mathcal{M}:=\mathbb{R}^{4}_{(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})}\setminus\{r<r_{0}\}

where
r
is the usual radial function,
\theta
and
\phi^{*}
are the standard spherical coordinates
defined by the relations
x^{1}=r\sin\theta\cos\phi^{*}
,
x^{2}=r\sin\theta\sin\phi^{*}
,
x^{3}=r\cos\theta
, and we relate
r=r(r_{BL},\theta,\phi^{*})

where this function in particular has the property that for

r_{BL}\leq R/2
we have
r=r_{BL}
. For a suitable choice
of
r
(see
[
DL17
]
)
the fixed-
r
spheres for
r\geq R
are then the spheres of a double null foliation.
In particular, there are two smooth functions
u(r,t^{*})
and
v(r,t^{*})
whose level sets are null
hypersurfaces in
r\geq R
and such that
u(R,0)=0
.

We have defined thus our Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
with boundary.
The manifold can be seen to be time-orientable and we choose the unique time orientation
making future pointing
the Kerr star coordinate vector
\partial_{t^{*}}
at some point of sufficiently large
r
, say

\partial_{t^{*}}(r=4M,t=0,\theta=0,\phi=0)
(for which the vector is indeed timelike).

Note that
\mathcal{S}:=\{r=r_{0}\}=\partial\mathcal{M}
is a spacelike
hypersurface with respect to
g_{a,M}
, and under our choice of time orientation it constitutes
a future boundary of
\mathcal{M}
. The null hypersurface
r=r_{+}
will be denoted
by
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and represents the event horizon.

We define finally the part spacelike, part null hypersurface

\Sigma_{0}=(\{t^{*}=0\}\cap\{r\leq R\})\cup(\{u=0\}\cap\{r\geq R\}).

2.1.2
The Killing fields
T
and
\Omega_{1}
and the ergoregion

The stationary and axisymmetric Killing fields will be denoted
T
and
\Omega_{1}
and correspond to

\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{\phi}
of Boyer–Lindquist coordinates (wherever these are defined) and
to
\partial_{t^{*}}
and
\partial_{\phi^{*}}
of Kerr star coordinates.
We recall that while the span of
T
and
\Omega_{1}
is timelike in
r>r_{+}
, there is no single
Killing
vector field in the span of
T
and
\Omega_{1}
which is globally timelike in
r>r_{+}

(unless
a=0
in which case
T
is such a vector field).

We define the
(extended) ergoregion
to be the subset of
\mathcal{M}
defined by

\{x\in\mathcal{M}:g(T,T)\geq 0\}=\{r\leq M+\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\}.

(2.2)

In particular, if
r>2M+\epsilon
, then
-g(T,T)\geq b(\epsilon)>0
.
Note that by our choice of time orientation,
T
is future directed outside the ergoregion.
(Note that in the
a=0
case, this “extended ergoregion” (
2.2
)
coincides exactly with the
part of
\mathcal{M}
in the black hole interior
r_{0}\leq r\leq 2M
, including the horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.)

For points outside the horizon but lying in the ergoregion (
2.2
),
it is clear that we may make the following statement:
Fixing any

r_{\rm fixed}>r_{+}
and restricted to
\{r_{\rm fixed}-\epsilon\leq r\leq r_{\rm fixed}+\epsilon\}
for small enough
\epsilon>0
,
we may indeed find a timelike vector field in the span of

T
and
\Omega_{1}
. This fact will play an important role in Section
3.4
.

Finally, we note that on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(where
r=r_{+}
) the so-called Hawking vector field

Z:=T+\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}\Omega_{1}

(2.3)

lies in the direction of the future null generator.

2.1.3
Other vector fields and the commutation operators
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
,

\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}

and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}

All vector fields to be described below are translation invariant with respect to both
T
and
\Omega_{1}
.

Given
r_{0}<r_{+}<r_{1}<r_{2}
all sufficiently close to
r_{+}
, recall from
[
DHRT22
]
that we may define an additional vector field
Y
, supported in
r\leq r_{1}+(r_{2}-r_{1})/2
and
capturing the redshift properties in a neighbourhood
of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The vectors
Y
,
Z
(defined by (
2.3
))
and a standard basis of rotation vector fields
\Omega_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,3

(defined
by the usual formulas with respect to
(\theta,\phi^{*})
coordinates), span the tangent space
in
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}+(r_{2}-r_{1})/4
.

We recall also from
[
DHRT22
]

the globally defined spanning set of vector fields
L
,
\underline{L}
,

\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
,
\Omega_{3}
,
the notation
|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}:=\sum_{i}r^{-2}|\Omega_{i}\psi|^{2}
,
and the fact that the expression

|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}

is a reference coercive expression on first derivatives of
\psi
.

We also recall our two sets of commutation operators from
[
DHRT22
]
,
the set
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
:

\mathfrak{D}_{1}=T,\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{2}=\Omega_{1},\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{3}=Y,\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{4}=\zeta L,\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{5}=\zeta\underline{L},\qquad\mathfrak{D}_{5+i}=(\zeta+\hat{\zeta})\Omega_{i},i=1,\ldots,3

and the set

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
:

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}=L,\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}=\underline{L},\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{2+i}=\Omega_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,3.

Here
\zeta
is a smooth cutoff such that
\zeta=1
for
r\geq 3R/4
and
\zeta=0
for
r\leq R/2

while
\hat{\zeta}
is a smooth cutoff such that
\hat{\zeta}=1
for
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}+(r_{2}-r_{1})/4

and
\hat{\zeta}=0
for
r\geq r_{1}+(r_{2}-r_{1})/2
.
We define the multi-index notation

\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}=\mathfrak{D}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\mathfrak{D}_{8}^{k_{8}},\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{5}^{k_{5}}

and denote
|{\bf k}|=\sum k_{i}
.

We will here introduce also the notation

\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}=T,\qquad\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}=\Omega_{1},\qquad\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}=\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{k_{2}}

(2.4)

for a set of operators involving commutation only with the Killing directions.

The significance of the untilded
\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
and

\mathfrak{D}_{i}
is that they have better commutation
properties with
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
(the former in fact commute exactly), while
the tilded
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
represent
a complete spanning set of the tangent
space at every point.
Thus, it is typically the
tilded
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
which will appear in our basic energies (see below), but
in the course of our proofs we will commute equation (
1.1
) only
with the untilded
\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
or
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
,
relying on additional elliptic estimates to recover estimates for derivatives in the remaining
directions in the span of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
.

2.1.4
Hypersurfaces, spacetime regions and volume forms

We may foliate our manifold
\mathcal{M}
by the hypersurfaces
\Sigma(\tau)

\Sigma(\tau):=(\{t^{*}=\tau\}\cap\{r\leq R\})\cup(\{u=\tau\}\cap\{r\geq R\})

which
intersect
the boundary
\mathcal{S}
transversally.
Note that
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}
is spacelike while
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}
is null
and these are translates of
\Sigma_{0}
by the one-parameter family of diffeomorphisms
generated by
T
.

In addition, the region
r\geq R
is foliated by translation invariant “ingoing” null cones

\underline{C}_{v}

parametrised by the smooth function
v
defined on
r\geq R
referred
to already in Section
2.1.1

and the vector fields
\underline{L}
,
\Omega_{i}
mentioned above
are tangent to
\underline{C}_{v}

and
\underline{L}
is in the direction of the null generator and satisfies

g(\underline{L},T)\sim-1
,
g(L,\underline{L})=-1
in
r\geq R
.
We define
\tau(v)
by the relation
\underline{C}_{v}\cap\{r=R\}=\Sigma(\tau(v))\cap\{r=R\}
.

We introduce the notations

\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}):=\cup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\Sigma(\tau),

\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{0}):=\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

and, for

\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)
,

\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v):=\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\setminus\bigcup_{\tilde{v}>v}\underline{C}_{\tilde{v}},

\Sigma(\tau,v):=\Sigma(\tau)\setminus\bigcup_{\tilde{v}>v}\underline{C}_{\tilde{v}}.

The spacetime region
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)
is a compact subset of spacetime
with past boundary
\Sigma(\tau_{0},v)

and future boundary
\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cup\Sigma(\tau_{1},v)\cup\underline{C}_{v}
.

The volume form of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
may be expressed as

dV_{\mathcal{M}}=\upsilon(r,\theta,\phi^{*})d\tau\,r^{2}dr\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi^{*}

and, for
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}
, with the choice
L
for the null normal, as

dV_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}}:=\tilde{\upsilon}(r,\theta,\phi^{*})r^{2}dr\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi^{*}

and for
\underline{C}_{v}
, with the choice
\underline{L}
for the null normal, as

dV_{\underline{C}_{v}}:=\tilde{\tilde{\upsilon}}(r,\theta,\phi^{*})r^{2}dr\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi^{*},

where

\upsilon\sim\tilde{\upsilon}\sim\tilde{\tilde{\upsilon}}\sim 1.

The volume
form of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a.M})
is related to the volume form of
\Sigma(\tau)

dV_{\mathcal{M}}\sim d\tau\,dV_{\Sigma(\tau)},

where
\sim
is interpreted for
4
-forms in the obvious sense.

Let us note that in the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq R/2
, where in particular
r=r_{BL}
, the spacetime volume form may be reexpressed
in Kerr-star coordinates as

dV_{\mathcal{M}}=(r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)dt^{*}\,dr\,\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi^{*},

(2.5)

or alternatively, in the region
r_{+}<r\leq R/2
,
in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates as

dV_{\mathcal{M}}=(r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)dt\,dr\,\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi.

Note that when volume forms are omitted from integrals, the above induced volume forms from the metric
g_{a,M}
will be
understood, unless otherwise noted.

2.1.5
Note on parameters and constants

The above constructions already referred to some
r
-parameters

r_{0}<r_{+}<r_{1}<r_{2}<R,

where
r_{0}
,
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
may be taken arbitrarily close to
r_{+}

and
R
may be taken arbitrarily large.
We will in fact only fix these parameters later in the paper as they will be constrained
by certain additional parameters to be introduced in the course of our arguments.
We give the full list of
r
-parameters we will need (in increasing order) in Table
1
,
including comments about their significance.
The role of most parameters has been discussed in
[
DHRT22
]
.
We note the important new parameters
r_{\rm pot}<R_{\rm pot}<R_{\rm freq}
which will
be constrained in the process of our frequency analysis in Appendix
A
.
We will then require
r_{2}<r_{\rm pot}
and
R_{\rm freq}\leq R/4
,
so that in particular (
2.5
) holds
in
r\leq R_{\rm freq}
.

Table 1:

r
-parameters adapted from
[

]

for

for

;

is null,
\underline{C}_{v}\subset\{r\geq R\}

As in
[
DHRT22
]
, let us also fix a parameter

0<\delta<\frac{1}{10}.

(2.6)

This will be related to the degeneration of the
r^{p}
method
[
DR09
]

as one approaches
p=0

and
p=2
, i.e. we will constrain
p
to be
p=0
or
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
.

We will use
k
to denote integers which will parametrise number of derivatives.

We will follow the conventions for constants from
[
DHRT22
]
:
Namely, in inequalities, we denote by
C
and
c
generic positive constants, which may be chosen so as to
eventually
(see remarks below!) depend only on (a) the Kerr parameters
a
,
M

(b) the choice (
2.6
) of
\delta
(when
\delta
-dependent
quantities or energies are invoked), and (c)
if there is
k
-dependence
in the relevant statement, also on
k
.
(We use
C
for large constants and
c
for small constants.)
The notation
[\ldots]\lesssim[\ldots]
will be used to denote
[\ldots]\leq C[\ldots]
,
for a
C
as above.

In addition to the above
r
-parameters and the parameter
\delta
,
in the course of our proofs we will need to introduce a large number of additional fixed parameters,
which again, can eventually be chosen to depend only on
a
and
M
, but will often
only be fixed later in the paper, for instance:

b_{\rm trap},b_{\rm elliptic},\gamma_{\rm elliptic}

(2.7)

which are used in characterising some frequency ranges or

E,e_{\rm red},B_{\rm indep}

(2.8)

which appear as parameters in the construction of currents.

We shall explicitly describe the order in which parameters such
as (
2.7
) or (
2.8
)
must be chosen in the text.
We warn the reader that before (
2.7
) are fixed, some generic constants
C
may depend
also on these parameters.

Moreover, when we write that a generic constant
C
depends on other quantities,
C=C(x,y,\ldots)
, we mean that this dependence
on
x
,
y
, etc. is
in addition
to
a
and
M
(and
k
,
\delta
as appropriate) and potentially any parameters as above not yet chosen.

Let us make an explicit remark already about two particular additional classes of such parameters:
Parameters which are small
and which often multiply other constants
on the right hand
side of estimates will typically
be denoted
by variants of
\epsilon
and

\varepsilon
. The parameters
\epsilon
are related to showing coercivity and other properties
of our current constructions while the parameters
\varepsilon
appear in smallness
assumptions for our norms.

Regarding
\epsilon
-parameters (for instance
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
from (
3.55
)), until these are fixed, then according to our conventions above,
generic constants
c
,
C
may depend
(unfavourably)
also on these as
\epsilon\to 0
. We will have to track this dependence carefully.
To assist the reader, we will always adhere to the following additional convention for
\epsilon
-parameters: (1) Small constants
c
may always be chosen independent of
\epsilon
and
(2) large constants
C
(explicitly displayed or implicit in a
\lesssim
)
multiplying
such an
\epsilon
can always be chosen
independent of
\epsilon
. (When appealing to this convention, we will in particular explain
why the constants of our inequalities can indeed be chosen as claimed.)
This will allow us to absorb such
C\epsilon
terms (for small enough
\epsilon
), thus allowing
us to eventually fix
\epsilon
depending only on
a
,
M
(and
k
,
\delta
as appropriate), eliminating thus
the
\epsilon
-dependence of other generic constants
C
.

For
\varepsilon
-parameters, on the other hand, there will never be unfavourable dependence
of generic constants
C
in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

2.2
Covariant energy identities

As in
[
DHRT22
]
, covariant energy identities will play a fundamental role in the present
paper as these will be applied at highest order. We review briefly the formalism to set the notation.
For a much more general discussion of such identities, see
[
Chr00
]
.

Our physical space identities are associated
to a quadruple
(V,w,q,\varpi)
. Here,
V^{\mu}
is a vector field on
\mathcal{M}
,
w
is a scalar function, and
q_{\mu}
,
\varpi_{\mu\nu}
are
1
-forms and
2
-forms, respectively.
Given
(V,w,q,\varpi)
, a general Lorentzian metric
g
on
\mathcal{M}
and a suitably regular
(complex-valued)
function
\psi
,
define

\displaystyle J^{V,w,q,\varpi}_{\mu}[g,\psi]

\displaystyle:=T_{\mu\nu}[g,\psi]V^{\nu}+w{\rm Re}(\psi\overline{\partial_{\mu}\psi})+q_{\mu}|\psi|^{2}+*d\big(|\psi|^{2}\varpi\big)_{\mu}\,,

(2.9)

\displaystyle K^{V,w,q}[g,\psi]

\displaystyle:=\pi^{V}_{\mu\nu}[g]T^{\mu\nu}[g,\psi]+\nabla^{\mu}w{\rm Re}(\psi\overline{\partial_{\mu}\psi})+w\nabla^{\mu}\psi\overline{\partial_{\mu}\psi}+\nabla^{\mu}q_{\mu}|\psi|^{2}+2{\rm Re}(\psi q_{\mu}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\psi)\,,

(2.10)

\displaystyle H^{V,w}[\psi]

\displaystyle:=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi+w\psi\,,

(2.11)

where

T_{\mu\nu}[g,\psi]:={\rm Re}(\partial_{\mu}\psi\overline{\partial_{\nu}\psi})-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{\alpha}\psi\overline{\partial_{\alpha}\psi},\qquad\pi^{X}_{\mu\nu}[g]:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{L}_{X}g)_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}X_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}X_{\mu}),

and where
*\colon\Lambda^{3}\mathcal{M}\to\Lambda^{1}\mathcal{M}
is
the Hodge star operator corresponding to
g
.
(Note we here allow complex valued
\psi
, as opposed to
[
DHRT22
]
where everything
was assumed real valued. This is primarily for the convenience of Fourier analysis.)
We will often denote the
g
dependence with a subscript, i.e.
K_{g}^{V,w,q}[\psi]
, etc.

For a general function
\psi
, the current
J^{V,w,q,\varpi}_{\mu}
satisfies
the following fundamental divergence identity

\nabla_{g}^{\mu}J^{V,w,q,\varpi}_{\mu}[g,\psi]=K^{V,w,q}[g,\psi]+{\rm Re}\left(H^{V,w}[\psi]\overline{\Box_{g}\psi}\right),

(2.12)

which upon integration in the region
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma(\tau_{1},v)}J[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}+\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}J[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}+\int_{\underline{C}_{v}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}J[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)}K[\psi]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma(\tau_{0},v)}J[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}-\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)}{\rm Re}\left(H[\psi]\overline{\Box_{g}\psi}\right),

(2.13)

where the normals, contractions and volume forms are with respect to the metric
g
.
We will always apply (
2.13
) either with the exact
Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
or with metrics
g
sufficiently
close to Kerr
satisfying moreover
g=g_{a,M}
in
r\geq R/2
. Thus,

\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}
will be spacelike
with respect to
g
and

\underline{C}_{v}
and
\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}

will be null,
and where
moreover we may use the null generators
L
and
\underline{L}
to fix the normal (and volume form) on the null parts
of
\Sigma(\tau)
and on
\underline{C}_{v}
, respectively. See Section
2.1.4
.

The currents we shall define in this paper will essentially all arise more naturally
as suitable combinations of “twisted currents”, which are expressed in terms of a “twisted” energy
momentum tensor and “twisted derivatives”.
These are reviewed in Appendix
A.2
.
The total currents can always be re-expressed however in the form given above.

2.3
Energies and their properties

We recall now the energies defined in
[
DHRT22
]
specialised to the
subextremal Kerr manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
.

2.3.1
Non-degenerate boundary and bulk energies

In this section, we will recall the definitions of the non-degenerate boundary and bulk energies.

Let us be given
k\geq 0
,
\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}
,
v
,
and a (complex-valued) function
\psi
defined on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

We recall first the “unweighted” energies:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\underline{C}_{v}\cap\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.16)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}r^{-1-\delta}\left(|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}\right)+r^{-3-\delta}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}.

(2.17)

The
′
on the quantity defined in (
2.17
) denotes that this quantity typically appears in
bulk
integrals, i.e.
integrated
in
\tau
. Note that
k
refers to the number of commutations, so energies
with a
k
subscript are of order
k+1
in
\psi
. Note moreover that we use the tilded

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{i}
as commutators. All integrals are with respect
to the volume forms induced by the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
according to the conventions
recalled in Section
2.1.4
.
The term “unweighted” refers to the fact that the above expressions are naturally related to boundary and bulk terms of energy currents which near infinity are controlled by the boundary terms of the current
J^{T}
associated with the stationary vector field
T
, without additional weights.

We will often omit the
[\psi]
from (
2.14
)–(
2.17
)
and the definitions which follow,
when it is understood which
\psi
we are
applying this to.

Let us also introduce the notations

\displaystyle{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}{}_{r\leq r_{1}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq r_{1}\}}|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.18)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}{}_{r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r_{1}\leq r\leq r_{2}\}}|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}|L{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}|L{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|{\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}.

(2.21)

For global existence and stability it is essential to control quantities with stronger weights
at infinity than those corresponding to the current
J^{T}
. These will correspond
to the weights of currents associated to the so-called
r^{p}
method
[
DR09
]
,
which is
a hierarchy of estimates for various values of
p
.
We recall thus the
p
-weighted analogues of the above for
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
,
which are defined as below:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p}|r^{-1}L(r\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi)|^{2}+r^{\frac{p}{2}}|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{\frac{p}{2}-2}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.22)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau)[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\underline{C}_{v}\cap\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}r^{p}|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+r^{p-2}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}r^{p-1}\left(|r^{-1}L(r\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi)|^{2}+|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}\right)+r^{p-3}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}.

(2.24)

We recall the fundamental hierarchical relations at the heart of the
r^{p}
method:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p^{\prime})$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny{$k$}}}}{\mathcal{E}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p^{\prime})$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}{\rm\ \ for\ }p\geq p^{\prime}\geq\delta{\rm\ or\ }p^{\prime}=0,\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}{\rm\ for\ }p\geq 1+\delta,\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}{\rm\ for\ }p\geq 1.

(2.25)

2.3.2
Degenerate bulk energies and the degeneration functions
\chi
,
\tilde{\rho}

We recall from Section
1.1.3

that the scheme of
[
DHRT22
]
involved a hierarchy of degenerate
energies, where
the
degeneration was captured by a set of functions
\chi
,
\rho
,
\tilde{\rho}
and
\xi
,
appearing in the blackbox assumptions 1. and 2.,
whose support properties are moreover non-trivially related.

In the present paper we shall not make use of
\xi
,
\rho
,
while we may define
\chi

to be a function

\chi=0\text{\ in\ }(r_{\rm pot},R_{\rm pot}),\qquad\chi=1\text{\ in\ }[r_{0},r_{\rm pot}]\cup[R_{\rm pot},\infty)

(2.26)

and

\tilde{\rho}=0\text{\ in\ }[r_{0},R_{\rm pot}),\qquad\tilde{\rho}=1\text{\ in\ }[R_{\rm pot},\infty)

(2.27)

where
r_{+}<r_{\rm pot}<2.01M<5M<R_{\rm pot}
are parameters chosen at the beginning of Section
A.1.5
.

We recall from
[
DHRT22
]
the definitions:

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}r^{-1-\delta}\chi(r)\left(|L\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}\right),

(2.28)

\displaystyle{}^{\tilde{\rho}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-3\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}\tilde{\rho}(r)r^{-3-\delta}|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2},

(2.29)

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle:={}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p-1}\left(|r^{-1}L(r\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi)|^{2}+|L\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}\right)+r^{p-3}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}.

(2.30)

We remind the reader that unlike the boundary and bulk energies of
Section
2.3.1
, the energies above,
which are to
appear in bulk integrals only
, have been defined with respect to the untilded
\mathfrak{D}^{i}

operators (except for the terms only supported in
r\geq R
, for which the two definitions
would have been equivalent and it is more convenient to retain
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{i}
).

2.3.3
Master energies

It was convenient in
[
DHRT22
]
to bundle some of the energies together as “master energies”.
We define thus the following for
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq v(\tau)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left(\,\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+{}^{\tilde{\rho}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-3\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left(\,\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime}.

Note that we have retained the
\rho
notation on
{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}
for ease of comparison
with
[
DHRT22
]
, even though there is no longer a function
\rho
. Thus,
\chi
replaces

\rho
on the right hand side in the definition. Note that
{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

and
{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
now differ only with respect to lower order terms.
(Note however that in contrast to
[
DHRT22
]
, we included an extra
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})
term for convenience in the definition of
{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.)

For
p=0
, in the analogous definitions,
p-1
is replaced by
-1-\delta
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left(\,\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+{}^{\tilde{\rho}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-3\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu2$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left(\,\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime}.

As discussed in
[
DHRT22
]
, the case
p=0
is anomalous, and thus we will need
to also consider the following stronger energies which will appear
on the right hand side
of
p=0
estimates:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0+)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(\delta-1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0+)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left({}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(\delta-1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+{}^{\tilde{\rho}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-3\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime},

\displaystyle{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0+)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\sup_{v:\tau_{1}\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\left({}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(\delta-1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(\delta-1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\right)d\tau^{\prime}.

We recall also the general properties that
p^{\prime}\geq p
,
k^{\prime}\geq k

implies
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p^{\prime})$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny{$k^{\prime}$}}}}{\mathcal{X}}}\gtrsim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}
,
{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p^{\prime})$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny{$k^{\prime}$}}}}{\mathcal{X}}}\gtrsim{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}
,

{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p^{\prime})$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny{$k^{\prime}$}}}}{\mathcal{X}}}\gtrsim{}^{\rho}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}
, while

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}\lesssim{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}.

(2.31)

For the bounding of errors arising from pseudodifferential commutation,
it will be useful to define finally

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}{}^{*}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime})+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{r\leq R_{\rm freq}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

where the
r\leq R_{\rm freq}
subscript here denotes that the integrand in the definition
of the energy is to be restricted to that set.
Note that for
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}+1
, this satisfies

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}{}^{*}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\lesssim(\tau_{1}-\tau_{0})\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau^{\prime}).

(2.32)

We will use the notation
\ll\mkern-6.0muk

or
\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{\small$\ll$}}}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}

to denote some particular positive integer, depending on
k
,
which may be different on different instances of our use of the notation,
such that
\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{\small$\ll$}}}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}\leq k
, and in fact,
\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{\small$\ll$}}}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}
is “much less than
k
”,
provided
k
is sufficiently
large. In particular, for all positive integers
n
,
we assume there exists a
k(n)
for which
k\geq k(n)
implies
\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{\small$\ll$}}}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}\leq k-n
.

2.3.4
Sobolev inequalities and interpolation of
p
-weighted energies

We recall the elementary Sobolev and interpolation estimates
used in
[
DHRT22
]
.

Proposition 2.3.1

(Proposition 3.6.10 of
[
DHRT22
]
)
.

Let
\psi
be a smooth function defined on some neighbourhood of
\Sigma(\tau)
.
Then we have

\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-3}\sup_{x\in\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi(x)|^{2}\lesssim\min\left\{\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi],\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]\right\}.

(2.33)

Proposition 2.3.2

(Proposition 3.6.11 of
[
DHRT22
]
)
.

For
\delta
as fixed in (
2.6
), one has
the following interpolation inequalities:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(1-\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]\right)^{1-\delta}\left(\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(2-\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]\right)^{\delta},

(2.34)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(\delta-1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(-1\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu\delta)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]\right)^{\frac{1-\delta}{1+\delta}}\left(\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau)[\psi]\right)^{\frac{2\delta}{1+\delta}}.

(2.35)

2.3.5
Elliptic estimates

Finally, we recall the following elliptic estimates of
[
DHRT22
]
, specialised to Kerr:

Proposition 2.3.3

(Proposition 3.6.3 of
[
DHRT22
]
)
.

Fix
|a|<M
and
let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
denote the Kerr manifold.
Let
\psi
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

and let
\tau_{0}\leq\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau_{1}
.
Then for all
k\geq 1
and for all
r_{+}<r^{\prime}_{-}<r^{\prime}<r^{\prime\prime}<r^{\prime\prime}_{+}\leq R
,

\int_{\Sigma(\tau^{\prime})\cap\{r^{\prime}\leq r\leq r^{\prime\prime}\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k+1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf{{k}}}\psi|^{2}\\
\lesssim\int_{\Sigma(\tau^{\prime})\cap\{r^{\prime}_{-}\leq r\leq r^{\prime\prime}_{+}\}}\sum_{0\leq|{\bf k}|\leq k}\sum_{|{\bf k}^{\prime}|=1}|\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}{\mathfrak{D}}^{{\bf k}^{\prime}}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq 1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf{k}}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi|^{2},

(2.36)

where here
\lesssim
depends on the choice of
r_{+}<r^{\prime}_{-}<r^{\prime}<r^{\prime\prime}<r_{+}^{\prime\prime}
.
We also have the estimate, for all
r_{0}\leq r^{\prime}\leq r_{1}
,

\int_{\Sigma(\tau^{\prime})\cap\{r\geq r^{\prime}\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k+1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf{{k}}}\psi|^{2}\\
\lesssim\int_{\Sigma(\tau^{\prime})\cap\{r\geq r^{\prime}\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k+1}|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi|^{2}.

(2.37)

Note that the analogous statements to (
2.36
), (
2.37
)
with integration on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r^{\prime}\leq r\leq r^{\prime\prime}\}
, etc.,
follow immediately in view of the coarea formula.

In fact, even without assuming
r^{\prime\prime}_{+}\leq R
(i.e. allowing in particular
r^{\prime\prime}>R
), we still have the spacetime elliptic estimate:

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r^{\prime}\leq r\leq r^{\prime\prime}\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k+1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf{{k}}}\psi|^{2}\\
\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r^{\prime}_{-}\leq r\leq r^{\prime\prime}_{+}\}}\sum_{0\leq|{\bf k}|\leq k}\sum_{|{\bf k}^{\prime}|=1}|\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}{\mathfrak{D}}^{{\bf k}^{\prime}}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq 1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf{k}}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi|^{2},

(2.38)

where again
\lesssim
depends on the choice of
r_{+}<r^{\prime}_{-}<r^{\prime}<r^{\prime\prime}<r_{+}^{\prime\prime}
.

Remark 2.3.4
.

We remind the reader that (
2.38
) in the case
r^{\prime\prime}>R

must be proven directly as a spacetime estimate and not as a corollary of an estimate
of the type (
2.36
)
on fixed slices
\Sigma(\tau)
, precisely
because the
\Sigma(\tau)
slices are null in the region
r\geq R
.
(See the proof of Proposition 3.6.3 of
[
DHRT22
]
for more details.)

3
Integrated local energy decay estimates and physical space currents for
\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
on subextremal Kerr
|a|<M

In this section we will introduce the estimates and identities
concerning the linear wave operator
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
on subextremal Kerr
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
,
|a|<M

which as
discussed already in the introduction constitute the basis of our method.

The first estimate (Theorem
3.1
)
is the black box degenerate integrated local energy decay
estimate
for solutions
\psi
of
\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F

corresponding to black box assumption 1. of
[
DHRT22
]
.
We will state this in Section
3.1
. This estimate
was proven in
[
DRSR16
]
.

For the purposes of the remainder of the section, we will need to review
the geometric Fourier analysis with respect to the Killing fields
T
and
\Omega_{1}
and
the full
Carter separation
[
Car68
]
of
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
.
This will be done in Section
3.2
.
We will in particular introduce the notion of a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator
satisfying a rudimentary pseudodifferential calculus.

As discussed already in Section
1.2.2
, it turns out that we will need in fact a slight refinement of Theorem
3.1
,
including now elliptic estimates on certain non-trapped
frequency ranges, which can be defined with respect to Carter’s separation.
We will state that refinement in Section
3.3

as Theorem
3.2
.

The main new element of the present paper will be presented
in Section
3.4
. There, we shall introduce the set of
coercive currents
J^{{\rm main},n}
,
K^{{\rm main},n}
, discussed already in
Section
1.2.3
,
which will replace the role of assumption 2. of
[
DHRT22
]
.
The coercivity properties of these currents hold only
when applied to a corresponding set of wave packets
defined by a finite set of frequency projection operators
P_{n}
, introduced here.
These operators may be understood as zeroth order
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential
operators in the formalism of Section
3.2
.
Moreover, the allowed errors to coercivity of
K^{{\rm main},n}

include highest order terms which can only be controlled by
the elliptic improvement of Theorem
3.2
. In order to capture this,
however, we must further frequency analyse our expressions
K^{{\rm main},n}

with respect to Carter’s separation. Since the very design of the currents is intimately
connected to showing this property, the precise definitions of the functions
constituting the currents
J^{{\rm main},n}
,
K^{{\rm main},n}

is in fact deferred to Appendix
A
,
which contains all arguments making use
of Carter’s separation. Thus, the proof of most of the results of Section
3.4

will depend on constructions and proofs
postponed to Appendix
A
.

Finally, in Section
3.5
we shall introduce additional currents
to obtain
improved coercivity properties near the
boundary
\mathcal{S}
and in the far-away region, further exploiting the red-shift property
and the
r^{p}
estimate hierarchy.

As defined in the sections above, the above currents all still depend on some free parameters which
must be taken large or small accordingly.
In Section
3.6
, we shall finalise our choice of all parameters appearing
in our currents, in particular fixing thus the choice of the
r
-parameters of
Section
2.1.5

so as to depend only on
a
,
M
.

3.1
The black box integrated local energy decay assumption for
subextremal Kerr
[
DRSR16
]

We review here the black box local energy decay assumption (statement 1.) required by
[
DHRT22
]
and proven in
[
DRSR16
]
for the Kerr case in the general
subextremal range
|a|<M
.
The statement will refer to the degeneration function
\chi
defined in Section
2.3.2
.
For the right hand side, it will also refer to a vector field
V_{p}
and function
w_{p}

which are defined in Appendix B.1 of
[
DHRT22
]
but may also be taken to be the
functions (with respect to the parametrisation of currents in Section
2.2
)
corresponding to the current
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}^{\rm total}
of Section
3.5
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Fix
|a|<M
and
let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
denote the Kerr manifold and let
\psi
satisfy

\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

For all
k\geq 0
, for all
0<\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
and for all

\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}
we have the following statement:

\displaystyle\sup_{v:\tau\leq\tau(v)}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{F}}}(v,\tau_{0},\tau)[\psi]

\displaystyle+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau)[\psi]+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]d\tau^{\prime}+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})[\psi]d\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\geq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\left(|V^{\mu}_{p}\partial_{\mu}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi)|+|w_{p}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|\right)|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}{F}|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}\left(|L\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\underline{L}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\not{\nabla}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|{\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}+\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}.

In the case
p=0
, an identical statement holds where we replace
p-1
by
-1-\delta
.

Proof.

The
p=0
,
k=0
,
F=0
statement is contained in the proof of
[
DRSR16
]
,
given our definition of
\chi
in (
2.26
)
and the relation of the parameters
r_{\rm pot}
,

R_{\rm pot}
(as these are finally chosen in
Section
A.1.5
) to the properties of the potential
V_{0}
.
For the statement for general
F
, see Theorem D.1 of
[
DHRT22
]
(where
note we only here apply the weaker version whose proof is contained in Section D).
Finally, to promote this statement to higher
k
and
0<\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
, see
Section 3.6 of
[
DHRT22
]
, in particular Remark 3.6.9.
∎

3.2
The
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
frequency analysis, superradiance
and Carter’s complete separation

The remainder of this section will need more precise statements which require
frequency analysis.
We review here the natural geometric frequency analysis
associated to the operator
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
.
We will distinguish between Fourier analysis with respect to Kerr star coordinates

t^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
, on the one hand, which just depends on the fact that
T
and
\Omega_{1}

are Killing
fields, and Carter’s full separation, which arises from the presence of an additional “hidden
symmetry”.

We begin in Section
3.2.1
with the definition of some basic spaces, cutoffs
and norms which will appear naturally when applying our frequency analysis.
The
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
frequency analysis and pseudodifferential calculus is then introduced
in Section
3.2.2
. This will in particular allow us to discuss superradiant
and non-superradiant frequencies in Section
3.2.3
.
We shall introduce an alternative Fourier transform with respect to Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
in Section
3.2.4
, which is used in our discussion of Carter’s complete separation,
given in Section
3.2.5
. Finally, we will collect some important Plancherel identities
in Section
3.2.6
.

3.2.1
Schwartz space, temporal cutoff functions
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}
and global mixed
Sobolev
norms

We will only apply Fourier analysis in the the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}
, for
a parameter
R_{\rm freq}\leq R/4
to be determined later.
Let us recall that we have an ambient Cartesian coordinate system
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
and
the Kerr star coordinates
(t^{*},\phi^{*},\theta,r)
, where
x^{0}=t^{*}
.
Let us use
\beta=(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3})
to denote multi-indices related to
the Cartesian coordinates and
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
to denote multi-indices
related to the
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
pair.

It will be natural to consider our Fourier based operators as defined in the Schwartz class with respect to
t^{*}=x^{0}

in the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}
, i.e. to define

\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}):=\{\Psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}:\forall\beta_{0},\tilde{\beta}\,\exists C_{\beta_{0},\tilde{\beta}}:\sup|t_{*}^{\beta_{0}}\partial^{\tilde{\beta}}\Psi|\leq C_{\beta_{0},\tilde{\beta}}\}\,.

(3.1)

To put smooth spacetime functions into the space (
3.1
), we will typically
cut off in time.
Given fixed
\tau_{0}+2\leq\tau_{1}
, we
introduce a cutoff
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}
which is
0
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
and

\tau\geq\tau_{1}

and
1
for
\tau_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}-\frac{1}{2}
, and where
the translates
\chi(\tau-\tau_{0})|_{[0,1/2]}

and
\chi(\tau-\tau_{1})_{[-1/2,0]}
are independent of
\tau_{0}
,
\tau_{1}
respectively.
Clearly, if
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}))
, then
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})
,
\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})
, etc.

We may also define the global
L^{2}
norm on (
3.1
)
by the expression:

\|\Psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}))}:=\int_{r_{0}}^{R_{\rm freq}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}|\Psi|^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)dr\,dt^{*}\,\sin\theta\,d\phi^{*}\,d\theta

and the global mixed Sobolev norm:

\|\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}))}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k,\,|\beta|\leq j}\|\partial_{t^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{\phi^{*}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x^{0}}^{\beta_{0}}\partial_{x^{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x^{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x^{3}}^{\beta_{3}}\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}.

(3.2)

We will also consider a similar norm on
\mathcal{S}
:

\|\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}(\mathcal{S})}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k,\,|\beta|\leq j}\|\partial_{t^{*}}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{\phi^{*}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x^{0}}^{\beta_{0}}\partial_{x^{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x^{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x^{3}}^{\beta_{3}}\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})},

(3.3)

as well as the restriction of (
3.2
) to other subsets of
\mathcal{M}
.
Let us note that by our definition of the ambient Cartesian coordinates in Section
2.1.1
in terms of Kerr star coordinates,
we have the commutation relations

[\partial_{t^{*}},\partial_{x^{\mu}}]=0,\qquad[\partial_{\phi^{*}},\partial_{x^{3}}]=0,

(3.4)

[\partial_{\phi^{*}},\partial_{x^{1}}]=\partial_{x^{2}},\qquad[\partial_{\phi^{*}},\partial_{x^{2}}]=-\partial_{x^{1}},

(3.5)

between the two sets of coordinate vector fields.

We may extend the above definitions to negative integers
k\leq 0
in the standard way, using the
equivalent chacterisation

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{|\alpha|=|k|,\,|\beta|\leq j}\|(1+|\omega|)^{{\rm sgn}(k)\alpha_{0}}(1+|m|)^{{\rm sgn}(k)\alpha_{1}}\mathfrak{F}[\partial_{x^{0}}^{\beta_{0}}\partial_{x^{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x^{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x^{3}}^{\beta_{3}}\Psi](\omega,m,r,\theta)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})L^{2}(r^{2}dr\sin\theta d\theta)},

etc.

3.2.2
The
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
frequency analysis and elementary pseudodifferential calculus

We define now
\mathfrak{F}
to be the Fourier transform operator
with respect to
t^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
,

\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\omega t^{*}}e^{-im\phi^{*}}\Psi(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)dt^{*}d\phi^{*}.

Denoting
\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}:=\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{S})
we have that

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}:\widetilde{\mathscr{S}}\to\mathscr{S}
is given by the inverse Fourier transform:

\mathfrak{F}^{-1}[U](t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-i\omega t^{*}}e^{im\phi^{*}}U(r,\theta,\omega,m)d\omega.

We moreover have the Plancherel formula

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\Psi|^{2}(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)d\phi^{*}dt^{*}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}|\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)|^{2}d\omega.

(3.7)

We have the relations (note the signs!):

\partial_{t^{*}}\Psi(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}\omega\,\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)e^{-i\omega t^{*}}e^{im\phi^{*}}d\omega,

\partial_{\phi^{*}}\Psi(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}m\,\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)e^{-i\omega t^{*}}e^{im\phi^{*}}d\omega

and the additional Plancherel formulae:

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{t^{*}}\Psi|^{2}(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)\,d\phi^{*}\,dt^{*}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}\omega^{2}\,|\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)|^{2}d\omega,

(3.8)

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{\phi^{*}}\Psi|^{2}(t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)\,d\phi^{*}\,dt^{*}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}m^{2}\,|\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)|^{2}d\omega.

(3.9)

We will consider
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operators
of the form

Q[\Psi](t^{*},\phi^{*},r,\theta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}q_{\rm symb}(\omega,m,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})e^{-i\omega t^{*}}e^{im\phi^{*}}\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)d\omega

(3.10)

where

|\partial_{x^{0}}^{\beta_{0}}\partial_{x^{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x^{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x^{3}}^{\beta_{3}}\partial_{\omega}^{\alpha_{0}}D_{m}^{\alpha_{1}}q_{\rm symb}(\omega,m,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})|\leq C_{\beta}(1+|\omega|+|m|)^{s-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}}.

(3.11)

In the above

D_{m}^{0}U:=U,\qquad D_{m}^{1}U(\omega,m,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}):=U(\omega,m,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})-U(\omega,m-1,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})

and for
\alpha_{1}\geq 2
,

D_{m}^{\alpha_{1}}:=D_{m}(D_{m}^{\alpha_{1}-1}U).

We call
s
the
order
of the operator. For convenience we will only consider
integer order
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. We will say that such an operator is

(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-
smoothing

if (
3.11
) holds for all
s<0
and
C_{\beta}(s)
.

We will need only the most rudimentary facts about the pseudo-differential calculus
for operators of the form (
3.10
).
We first note (cf. (
3.4
), (
3.5
)) the following
computation:

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
s
with symbol
q_{\rm symb}(\omega,m,x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
and let
\Psi\in\mathscr{S}
. Then we have the commutation formulae:

[\partial_{x^{0}},Q]\Psi=\tilde{Q}_{0}\Psi,\qquad[\partial_{x^{3}},Q]\Psi=\tilde{Q}_{3}\Psi,

(3.12)

[\partial_{x^{1}},Q]\Psi=\tilde{Q}_{1}\Psi-\hat{Q}\partial_{x^{2}}\Psi-\hat{Q}^{\prime}\partial_{x^{1}}\Psi,\qquad[\partial_{x^{2}},Q]\Psi=\tilde{Q}_{2}\Psi+\hat{Q}\partial_{x^{1}}\Psi+\hat{Q}^{\prime}\partial_{x^{2}}\Psi

(3.13)

where

\tilde{Q}_{j}
is a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
s
with symbol
\partial_{x^{j}}q_{\rm symb}
, for
j=0,\ldots,3
, while

\hat{Q}
,
\hat{Q}^{\prime}
are
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operators of order
s-1
, with symbols
defined respectively by

\frac{1}{2i}(D^{1}_{m+1}+D^{1}_{m})q_{\rm symb},\qquad\frac{1}{2}(D^{1}_{m+1}-D^{1}_{m})q_{\rm symb}.

Proof.

The commutation formulae (
3.12
) are immediate.
For (
3.13
), noting the relations
between coordinate vector fields of the two coordinate systems,
we then compute explicitly the commutators using also (
3.12
).
∎

We may now easily show the following propositions,
whose proofs, in view of the above lemma, are elementary adaptations
of standard results of the pseudodifferential calculus (see e.g. Chapter 6 of
[
Ste93
]
).

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
\hat{s}
. Then
for the global mixed Sobolev norms defined
by (
3.2
) or (
3.3
), we have

\|Q[\Psi]\|_{H^{k-\hat{s},j}}\lesssim C(Q,k,j)\|\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}}.

Thus, if
Q
is
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-smoothing, then

\|Q[\Psi]\|_{H^{k+s,j}}\lesssim C(Q,k,j,s)\|\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}}

(3.14)

for all
s>0
.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
\hat{s}
, and let
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}

be smooth compactly supported functions on
\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}

supported in
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and
\tau\geq\tau_{2}
, respectively, for some
\tau_{2}>\tau_{1}
.
Then
\mu_{1}Q\mu_{2}
is
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-smoothing
and the constant
C(\mu_{1}Q\mu_{2},k,j,s)
appearing in (
3.14
) can be bound by
C(Q,k,j,s,\tau_{2}-\tau_{1},\|\mu_{1}\|_{H^{0,\tilde{s}}},\|\mu_{2}\|_{H^{0,\tilde{s}}})
for an
\tilde{s}
depending only on

s
,
k
and
j
, where the
\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}
dependence of
C
is such that

C|\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}+1|^{\ell}<\infty
for all
\ell\geq 0
.

Proposition 3.2.4
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
s
and let
\mu
be a smooth function of compact support on
\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}
.
Then
[Q,\mu]
is of order
s-1
.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator of order
0
whose symbol

q_{\rm symb}=q_{\rm symb}(\omega,m)
is a function of frequency only. Let
D=\sum_{|\beta|\leq\tilde{k}}a_{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\beta}
where
\beta
is a multiindex and

\partial_{x^{\beta}}
denotes the differential operator

\partial^{\beta_{0}}_{x^{0}}\cdots\partial^{\beta_{3}}_{x^{3}}
with respect to the ambient
Cartesian structure. Suppose the coefficients
\{a_{\beta}\}
are smooth and compactly supported
in
\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}
}
. Then for Schwartz functions

\Psi
on
\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\|[Q,D]\Psi

\displaystyle\|_{H^{k+1,j-\tilde{k}}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}

\displaystyle\leq C(Q,k,\tilde{k},j)\|\{a_{\beta}\}\|_{H^{0,s}((\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}))}\sup_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\|\chi_{\tau,\tau+2}\Psi\|_{H^{k,j}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}

\displaystyle\qquad+C(Q,k,\tilde{k},j)\|\{a_{\beta}\}\|_{H^{0,s}((\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}))}\|\Psi\|_{H^{k-2,j}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}

(3.15)

for some
s
depending on
\tilde{k}
,
j
and
k
.

Proof.

Note that
the first term on the right hand side appears as a consequence of the standard Poisson bracket type commutator calculation, the bound of
Proposition
3.2.2
and the additional localisation to time intervals of size 2. The second term accounts for the error terms from the semi-classical commutation and the smoothing terms resulting from the localisation of the first term via Proposition
3.2.3
.
∎

For related pseudodifferential setups in Kerr and Kerr–de Sitter, see
[
HK23a
,
Mav24
]
.

3.2.3
Superradiant and non-superradiant frequencies

As discussed already in Section
1.2.1
, the
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
frequency analysis of Section
3.2.2

already allows us to
distinguish between so-called
superradiant
and
non-superradiant

frequencies.

We say a frequency pair
(\omega,m)
is
superradiant
if

m\neq 0\qquad{\text{and}}\qquad 0<\frac{\omega}{am}<\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}}.

(3.16)

Otherwise, i.e. if

a=0,\qquad{\text{or}}\qquad m=0,\qquad{\text{or}}\qquad\frac{\omega}{am}\leq 0,\qquad{\text{or}}\qquad\frac{\omega}{am}\geq\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}},

(3.17)

we say that
(\omega,m)
is
non-superradiant
.

We recall that the quantity
\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}
represents the
angular velocity

of the horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
and the null generator of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
can be expressed as (
2.3
).
The significance of (
3.16
) can be understood by considering the
J^{T}_{g_{a,M}}
flux
through
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, where
J^{T}_{g_{a,M}}
is the current (
2.9
) corresponding
to
V=T
and all other objects
0
.

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Let
\Psi
be a function in the space (
3.1
)
such that
\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r_{+},\theta,\omega,m)
is supported
in the non-superradiant frequencies (
3.17
).
Then

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}J^{T}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\geq 0.

Proof.

This follows from the Plancherel identities (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) and the fact that (
2.3
) is future causal.
∎

In the language of the pseudodifferential calculus of Section
3.2.2
,
we have the following
immediate corollary:

Corollary 3.2.7
.

Let
Q
be a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operator in the sense of Section
3.2.2
whose symbol is supported in (
3.17
),
let
\psi\in\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}

denote the cutoff of Section
3.2.1
. Then

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}J^{T}_{g_{a,M}}[Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\geq 0.

3.2.4
The Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}_{BL}
with respect
to Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

Let us note that, restricted to
r>r_{+}
, we could also define the Fourier transform replacing

t^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
with the Boyer–Lindquist
t
and
\phi
. We will denote
this by
\mathfrak{F}_{BL}
, i.e. we have

\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\omega t}e^{-im\phi}\Psi(t,\phi,r,\theta)dtd\phi.

(3.18)

Note that
\mathfrak{F}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)
and

\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)
differ by a phase depending on
(r,t^{*})
which
is however poorly behaved at
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Note that with respect to
\mathfrak{F}_{BL}
, we have the relations (note again the signs!)

\partial_{t}\Psi(t,\phi,r,\theta)=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}\omega\,\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\phi}d\omega,

\partial_{\phi}\Psi(t,\phi,r,\theta)=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}m\,\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\phi}d\omega

and the Plancherel relations (cf. (
3.8
), (
3.9
)):

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{t}\Psi|^{2}(t,\phi,r,\theta)\,d\phi\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}\omega^{2}\,|\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)|^{2}d\omega,

(3.19)

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{\phi}\Psi|^{2}(t,\phi,r,\theta)\,d\phi\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m}m^{2}\,|\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\theta,\omega,m)|^{2}d\omega.

(3.20)

As we shall see,
it will be convenient to use (
3.18
) when invoking Carter’s separation in the section
immediately following.

3.2.5
Carter’s complete separation and the radial ODE

It is remarkable that the wave operator
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
fully separates,
reducing its study to the analysis of ordinary differential equations with respect
to
r
.
We refer the reader to the original work of Carter
[
Car68
]
.
The geometric origin of this fact is the presence of a hidden symmetry.

We review briefly this separation here:
We will denote by
\mathfrak{C}
the transform corresponding to Carter’s separation

\mathfrak{C}[U](r,\ell,\omega,m)=\int_{0}^{\pi}U(r,\theta,\omega,m)\,\overline{S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}}(\cos\theta)\,d\theta

and
\mathfrak{C}^{-1}
its inverse,

\mathfrak{C}^{-1}[u](r,\theta,\omega,m)=\sum_{\ell\geq|m|}U(r,\theta,\omega,m)\,S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}(\cos\theta)\,d\theta,

where
S_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}(\cos\theta)

denote the eigenfunctions of the second order self-adjoint operator

P(a\omega,m)U=-(\sin\theta)^{-1}\frac{d}{d\theta}\left(\sin\theta\frac{d}{d\theta}U\right)+(m^{2}(\sin\theta)^{-2}-(a\omega)^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)U,

(3.21)

whose eigenvalues we denote
\lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}\in\mathbb{R}
, where these are labeled
by the set
\{\ell\geq|m|\}
.
We will informally refer to the set

\{(\omega,m,\ell)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}:\ell\geq|m|\}

as
Carter frequency space
to distinguish it from the frequency space of
Section
3.2.2
.

We define

\Lambda_{\ell m}^{(a\omega)}:=\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2}.

(3.22)

It was shown in
[
DRSR16
]
that

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\geq|m|(|m|+1),

(3.23)

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\geq|am\omega|.

(3.24)

Following
[
DRSR16
]
, we will refer to triples
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
with
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
,

m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
3.23
)–(
3.24
)
as
admissible frequency triples
, irrespectively of whether
\Lambda
is related to
\omega

and
m
by (
3.22
).

We have that if
\psi
is a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, then

u_{\rm star}:=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}[\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi]

is a function of
(r,\omega,m,\ell)
, smooth in
r\in[r_{0},\infty)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
, then
u_{\rm star}(r,\omega,m,\ell)
satisfies for fixed
(\omega,m,\ell)

an ordinary differential equation with respect to
r
.

In this paper, we could work directly with the equation satisfied by
u_{\rm star}
.
To connect, however, with previous literature, and since we will only apply
Carter’s separation in the region
r>r_{+}
, we will work instead with:

u=u_{BL}:=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi]

and we will moreover consider this as a function of
r^{*}
,
defined in turn from
r
by the relations

\frac{dr^{*}}{dr}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta},\qquad r^{*}(3M)=0.

Note that
r^{*}\to-\infty
as
r\to r_{+}
while
r^{*}\to\infty
as
r\to\infty
.
The function
u
is smooth with respect to
r^{*}\in(-\infty,\infty)
and satisfies an ODE
which may be written

u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V)u=G

(3.25)

where

V:=\frac{4Mram\omega-a^{2}m^{2}+\Delta\Lambda}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{4}}\left(a^{2}\Delta+2Mr(r^{2}-a^{2})\right)

(3.26)

and

G:=\frac{\Delta\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}((r^{2}+a^{2}cos^{2}\theta)(\chi^{2}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}F+2\nabla_{g_{0}}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}\cdot\nabla_{g_{0}}\psi+(\Box_{g_{0}}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}})\psi)}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3/2}}

(3.27)

and
where
′
in fact denotes the derivative with respect to
r^{*}
.
Despite thinking of
u
as a function of
r^{*}
, we will often refer
to its values at various
r
by
u(r)
, whereby we mean
u(r^{*}(r))
.

We note that all information on
u_{\rm star}(r_{+})

is encoded in the limiting behaviour
u(r^{*})
as
r^{*}\to-\infty
.

Let us note that we may view
V
defined by (
3.26
) as a function of
r

and of Carter frequencies
(\omega,m,\ell)
, i.e. as a function

V(\omega,m,\ell,r)
, where we substitute (
3.22
) for
\Lambda
,
but alternatively, we may consider (
3.26
)
to define
V(\omega,m,\Lambda,r)

on the set of all admissible frequencies
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
, and
consider solutions of (
3.25
) for general such admissible frequencies.

Note finally the relations:

\partial_{t}\Psi(t,r,\theta,\phi)=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}\omega\,\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)S_{m\ell}(a\omega,\cos\theta)e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\phi}d\omega,

\partial_{\phi}\Psi(t,r,\theta,\phi)=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}m\,\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)S_{m\ell}(a\omega,\cos\theta)e^{-i\omega t}e^{im\phi}d\omega.

3.2.6
The Plancherel formulae for Carter’s separation

We collect here the Plancherel formulae corresponding to Carter’s separation
with respect to Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}
:

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\Psi|^{2}(t,r,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}|\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}d\omega,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{r}\Psi|^{2}(t,r,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}|\frac{d}{dr}\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}d\omega,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{t}\Psi|^{2}(t,r,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}\omega^{2}|\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}d\omega,

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\partial_{\phi}\Psi|^{2}(t,r,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell}m^{2}|\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}d\omega,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(|\partial_{\theta}\Psi|^{2}+\sin^{-2}\theta|\partial_{\phi}\Psi|^{2}\right)(t,r,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt+a^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\cos^{2}\theta|\partial_{t}\Psi|^{2}\sin\theta d\phi d\theta dt

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum\lambda_{m,\ell}^{(a\omega)}|\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\Psi](r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}d\omega.

3.3
A refined inhomogeneous estimate

The estimate provided by Theorem
3.1

has the advantage that it can be expressed in terms of a
physical space norm. This is at the expense, however, of losing some more precise control
contained in the results of
[
DRSR16
]
, which can only be expressed in terms
of the frequency decomposition of Section
3.2
.

While this less precise information was sufficient for
[
DHRT22
]
, in the present paper,
it will be essential to use the full results of
[
DRSR16
]
.
As discussed already in Section
1.2.2
,
we will in fact require a more precise estimate
than was proven in
[
DRSR16
]
.
Specifically, in regions of Carter
frequency space where the operator
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
is essentially elliptic for some
suitable interval of
r
, we
will require a corresponding gain of one order of differentiability in the estimate.
As we shall see below, this can easily be obtained from the methods of
[
DRSR16
]
.

In Section
3.3.1
, we shall first formulate a proposition capturing the precise frequency dependent statement of
[
DRSR16
]
. We shall then formulate our elliptic
improvement
in Section
3.3.2
. Finally, in Section
3.3.3
,
we shall combine the above two statements
with that of Theorem
3.1
,
giving Theorem
3.2
.
This latter theorem will then be the statement that
will be used in the remainder of the paper.

3.3.1
Capturing the precise result of
[
DRSR16
]

Let us recall the “trapped” frequency range
\mathcal{G}_{\natural}
of
[
DRSR16
]

\mathcal{G}_{\natural}:=\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\text{\ admissible\ }:|\omega|\geq\omega_{\rm high},\,\epsilon_{\rm width}\Lambda\leq\omega^{2}\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\Lambda,\,m\omega\notin(0,\frac{am^{2}}{2Mr_{+}}+\alpha\Lambda]\right\}

where
\omega_{\rm high}
,
\epsilon_{\rm width}
and
\alpha
were positive parameters.
We recall from
[
DRSR16
]
(see Proposition
A.1.2
of
Section
A.1.1
of the present paper), that
for
(\omega,m,\ell)\in\mathcal{G}_{\natural}
, the high frequency potential
V_{0}

(see (
A.2
)) has a unique interior maximum point
r^{0}_{\rm max}
. We denote
the corresponding value of
V_{0}
by
V_{\rm max}
. (Note,
V_{\rm max}
thus denotes
the maximum of
V_{0}
and not the maximum of
V
.)

The frequency range
\mathcal{G}_{\natural}
is actually larger than the true trapped
frequency range: In order for true trapping to occur, then, in addition to
V_{0}
having a maximum, the maximum value
V_{\rm max}
must be approximately equal to

\omega^{2}
. To capture thus genuine trapping,
we introduce a parameter
b_{\rm trap}>0
, to be determined later,
and define the smaller range:

\mathcal{G}_{\natural}(b_{\rm trap}):=\mathcal{G}_{\natural}\cap\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\text{\ admissible\ }:|V_{\rm max}-\omega^{2}|\leq b_{\rm trap}\omega^{2}\}.

(3.28)

We may now define a nonnegative function

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)
