with the following properties:

1.

For
(\omega,m,\Lambda)\not\in\mathcal{G}_{\natural}(b_{\rm trap})
,

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)=1
identically.

2.

For
(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}_{\natural}(b_{\rm trap})
, the function

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)
is smooth in
r
and
vanishes at the unique
maximum
r^{0}_{\rm max}
of the high frequency potential
V_{0}
(see (
A.2
) of
Section
A.1.1
).

3.

\chi^{\prime\prime}_{\natural}\gtrsim 1
wherever
\chi_{\natural}\leq 1/2
(where by our conventions
from Section
2.1.5

the constant implicit in
\gtrsim
is in particular independent of the frequency triple
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
).

Let
u_{\bf k}:=\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi]
.
To capture the more precise estimate proven in
[
DRSR16
]
,
we define the following quantity:

\displaystyle{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{-\infty}^{R_{\rm freq}^{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell\geq|m|}

\displaystyle\Delta r^{-2}\Big(|u^{\prime}_{\bf k}(r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)(\omega^{2}+r^{-1}m^{2}+r^{-1}\Lambda^{2})|u_{\bf k}(r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}\Big)dr^{*}d\omega,

where for
\Lambda
we plug in (
3.22
).

Remark 3.3.1
.

Concerning the
r^{-1}
and
r^{-2}
factors above:
Recall from Section
2.1.5
that the
C
implicit in
\lesssim
will eventually
depend only
a
and
M
(and
k
), but at this point in the paper may depend in addition
on parameters not yet fixed. Nonetheless, where possible, we try to state inequalities
to have as little as possible spurious dependence on other parameters, even if that dependence would be innocuous. For instance,
the
r^{-2}
and
r^{-1}
factors in the above definition has been included so that the
C

implicit in the inequality of Proposition
3.3.2
does not depend
on the choice of
R_{\rm freq}
. Our choice to include other (often non-sharp) powers of
r
in estimates to follow can be understood in a similar spirit.

We have the following:

Proposition 3.3.2
.

Fix
|a|<M
,
let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
denote the Kerr manifold and let
\psi
satisfy

\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Then

{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r_{1}\leq r\leq R\}}|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}F|^{2}.

(3.29)

Proof.

Were
\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)
defined as above with
\mathcal{G}_{\natural}

replacing
\mathcal{G}_{\natural}(b_{\rm trap})

then the
statement would in fact be contained in
[
DRSR16
]
. The improvement
with the definition of
\chi_{\natural}
as given (i.e. with

\mathcal{G}_{\natural}(b_{\rm trap})
) is straightforward.
(We will in fact show explicitly how to obtain it in
Remark
A.3.2
of Section
A.3
,
immediately after
the proof of the more important improvement to be discussed in
Section
3.3.2
below.)
∎

Remark 3.3.3
.

We
note that, according to our convention from Section
2.1.5
, the
constant implicit in (
3.29
) indeed depends also on
b_{\rm trap}
, until this is fixed
(in Section
A.4.4
).

3.3.2
Gaining a derivative in the elliptic frequency ranges

The above estimate of Proposition
3.3.2
, together with
Theorem
3.1
, is still not
quite sufficient to handle the type of error terms which will occur in our highest order
estimates. For this we need an additional improvement, which will be
stated below as Proposition
3.3.4
and proven in Section
A.3
,
using the methods of
[
DRSR16
]
.
This improvement concerns estimates which gain one derivative when
localised to regions in Carter frequency space and in physical space
where the wave
operator is in fact elliptic.

Given parameters
\gamma_{\rm elliptic}>0
,

b_{\rm elliptic}>0
, to be determined later,
and given a frequency pair
(\omega,m)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}

let us define

r_{\rm elliptic}(\omega,m):=\begin{cases}r_{+},&\text{\ if\ }a=0\text{\ or\ }m=0\text{\ or\ }|\omega/am|\geq\gamma_{\rm elliptic}\\
2.03M&\text{\ if\ }a\neq 0,m\neq 0\text{\ and\ }|\omega/am|<\gamma_{\rm elliptic}.\\
\end{cases}

(3.30)

Given admissible frequencies
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
, we consider the
r
-range

{\bf R}_{\rm elliptic}(\omega,m,\Lambda):=\{r:V_{0}\geq(1+b_{\rm elliptic})\omega^{2}\}\cap\{r_{\rm elliptic}(\omega,m)<r\leq 1.2R_{\rm pot}\},

(3.31)

where
V_{0}
is the high frequency potential (see (
A.2
)). Let

\iota_{\rm elliptic}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)

denote the indicator function of the set (
3.31
).

Let
u_{\bf k}:=\mathfrak{C}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{BL}[\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi]
.
We
may now define
the following
integrated quantity (localised in both physical and Carter frequency space)

{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]:=\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{-\infty}^{(1.2R_{\rm pot})^{*}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{m,\ell\geq|m|}\Delta r^{-2}\iota_{\rm elliptic}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)(V_{0}-\omega^{2})^{2}|u_{\bf k}(r,\omega,m,\ell)|^{2}dr^{*}d\omega,

(3.32)

where for
\Lambda
we again plug in (
3.22
).
Let us note that the quantity (
3.32
) is formed
by applying
k
commutations (hence the
k
subscript which always labels
number of commutation operators applied), but is of “order”
k+2
in
certain frequency ranges, i.e. one order more than the quantity
{}^{\chi}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}
say, which
we recall is of total order
k+1
. Hence, the extra

[formula]

in the notation.

We may now state an improved estimate for this quantity:

Proposition 3.3.4
.

Fix
|a|<M
,
let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
denote the Kerr manifold
g_{a,M}
and let
\psi
satisfy

\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Then we have the estimate:

\displaystyle{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle\lesssim{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq 1.3R_{\rm pot}\}}|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}F|^{2}.

(3.33)

Proof.

See Section
A.3
.
∎

Remark 3.3.5
.

As with Remark
3.3.3
, we
note that, according to our convention from Section
2.1.5
, the
constant implicit in (
3.33
)
indeed depends also on
b_{\rm elliptic}
and
\gamma_{\rm elliptic}
, until these are fixed
(in Section
A.4.4
).

3.3.3
The final improved estimate

Let us introduce a new notation

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]:={}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]+{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi].

(3.34)

The symbol
{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}

is meant to combine all symbolic notations on the right hand side.

We emphasise that we do
not
necessarily have

{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau^{*}_{0},\tau^{*}_{1})[\psi]\leq{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],\qquad{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau^{*}_{0},\tau^{*}_{1})[\psi]\leq{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

(3.35)

due to the nonlocal definition of these terms.
Nonetheless, we see easily that for
\tau_{0}+1\leq\tau^{*}_{0}\leq\tilde{\tau}^{*}_{0}+1\leq\tau_{1}^{*}\leq\tau_{1}-1
, we have

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau^{*}_{0},\tau^{*}_{1})[\psi]\lesssim{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],

and more generally

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\hat{\chi}(\tau)\psi]\lesssim{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],

(3.36)

where
\hat{\chi}(\tau)
is a general cutoff function, and the constant implicit in
\lesssim
depends
on derivatives of
\hat{\chi}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Fix
|a|<M
,
let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
denote the Kerr manifold and let
\psi
satisfy

\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=F
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

For all
k\geq 1
, and for
p=0
or
0<\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
and for all

\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}
,
we have the following statement:

\displaystyle{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

\displaystyle\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau_{0})[\psi]+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\geq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\left(|V^{\mu}_{p}\partial_{\mu}({\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi)|+|w_{p}{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi|\right)|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}{F}|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k}\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}\left(|L\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\underline{L}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\not{\nabla}\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|+|\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi|\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}|{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\leq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|{\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}+\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma(\tau)\cap\{r\leq R\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|\leq k-1}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}F|^{2}.

Proof.

This follows immediately putting together Theorem
3.1
and
Propositions
3.3.2
and
3.3.4
.
∎

3.4
The projections
P_{n}
, the currents
J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}
,

K_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}
and generalised coercivity statements

The second ingredient of this paper, discussed already in Section
1.2.3
, will concern a new coercivity statement for
a finite set of natural currents

J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n},\qquad K_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n},\qquad n=0,\ldots,N,

(3.37)

associated
with
\Box_{g_{a,M}}
.
The currents (
3.37
)
are themselves defined purely in physical space, i.e. they are currents
precisely in the sense of Section
2.2
,
but for each
n
, their most general
coercivity
properties will
only be valid
restricted to the image of a projection operator
P_{n}
,
defined using the Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}
in
t^{*}
and
\phi^{*}
(but without using
Carter’s full separation operator
\mathfrak{C}
).
(Thus, in the language of Section
3.2.2
,

P_{n}
will be a zeroth order
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operators and will satisfy a standard
good commutation calculus.)
Moreover, coercivity of bulk terms
will only be valid modulo a highest order “error” term which in practice will be absorbed
via the refined statement Theorem
3.2
of Section
3.3
above. Thus, in general, in order to actually show this coercivity we will need to analyse our solution
using Carter’s frequency separation, even though the definition of the projection operators and
the currents are both
independent of the Carter frequency
\ell
.

We first define the projections
P_{n}
in Section
3.4.1
below.
We define the currents (
3.37
)
in Section
3.4.2
and address boundary coercivity,
while we treat bulk coercivity in Sections
3.4.3
and
3.4.4
.
The definition of the currents (
3.37
) depends
on choices of functions which, though defined independently of
Carter’s separation, are best understood
in the context of showing coercivity using Carter’s separation.
All constructions and proofs requiring Carter’s separation, however, will be deferred to Appendix
A
. Thus, the main results of this section will depend in particular
on a number of propositions whose proof has been relegated there

For convenience, in the remainder of Section
3
, we will assume

a\neq 0,

as our frequency decomposition will involve inverse powers of
a
.
We shall return to the general case in Section
4.1
, as well as the issue of
uniformity of our estimates in
a
. (See already Remark
5.0.1
.)

3.4.1
The frequency space covering
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and the
projections
P_{n}
,
n=0,\ldots,N

We define here the projection operators
P_{n}
.
The operators separate the superradiant and nonsuperradiant
parts of the solution and further decompose each into wave packets.
As discussed already in Section
1.2.3
,
the point of projection is two-fold:

(i)

For the non-superradiant part,
to localise the position of trapping in each packet to a fixed sufficiently small
r
-interval
on which there exists a timelike Killing field.

(ii)

For the superradiant part,
to ensure that the elliptic frequency region corresponding to
all frequencies in the wave packet contains a common

r
-interval.

It is also useful technically
to separate low
(\omega,m)
frequencies, but these in practice can usually be estimated
together with the superradiant part.

To define
P_{n}
, we first require a well chosen
open covering of
\mathbb{R}
by finitely many open intervals
I_{n}
,
n=1,\ldots,N
:

\mathbb{R}=\cup_{n=1}^{N}I_{n},

(3.38)

determined by Proposition
A.1.8
of Section
A.1.5
.
This will allow us to define an associated
open cover
\mathcal{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{N}
of

(\omega,m)
frequency space:

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}=\cup_{n=0}^{N}\mathcal{F}_{n}.

(3.39)

The operators
P_{n}
will then be defined so as to project to frequencies supported in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
with the help of a suitable partition of unity subordinate to the cover.

We proceed with the details.

Definition of the
(\omega,m
)-frequency space cover
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.

We introduce an open cover
\mathcal{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{N}

of
(\omega,m)
frequency space (
3.39
) defined as follows.

We define first

\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\{\omega^{2}+m^{2}<2B_{\rm low}\},

for some fixed
B_{\rm low}>0
satisfying

B_{\rm low}\leq\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\rm high}
(where
\omega_{\rm high}
is the parameter
appearing
in the definition of (
3.28
)).
We may in fact take
B_{\rm low}=1
provided we assume (without loss of generality) that

\omega_{\rm high}\geq 2
). Note that for
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
admissible with

(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
we thus have in particular
that

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)=1.

(3.40)

Consider now the specific
open covering (
3.38
) of
\mathbb{R}

determined by Proposition
A.1.8
of Section
A.1.5
,
consisting of intervals

I_{n}
,
n=1,\ldots,N
.

We now may define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{n}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\omega^{2}+m^{2}>B_{\rm low},\quad m\neq 0,\,\,\omega/(am)\in I_{n}\},\qquad n=1,\ldots,N-2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N-1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\omega^{2}+m^{2}>B_{\rm low},\quad m\neq 0,\,\,\omega/(am)\in I_{N-1}\text{\rm\ or\ }m=0,\,\omega/a<0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\omega^{2}+m^{2}>B_{\rm low},\quad m\neq 0,\,\,\omega/(am)\in I_{N}\text{\rm\ or\ }m=0,\,\omega/a>0\}.

Properties of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.

In view of the definition above,
the properties of
I_{n}
established in
Proposition
A.1.8
of Section
A.1.5
translate
immediately into the following properties of the
ranges
\mathcal{F}_{n}
:

•

The collection
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
n=0,\ldots,N
defined above indeed cover
frequency space
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, i.e. (
3.39
) holds. Moreover, replacing
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with a real valued variable
m\in\mathbb{R}

in their definition,
\mathcal{F}_{n}
can be viewed as an open covering of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
n=N_{s}+1,\ldots,N
, all
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}

are non-superradiant, i.e. satisfy (
3.17
),
and in fact
\omega/am
is uniformly bounded away from
0

and
\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}}
or else
m=0
(in which case we note that necessarily
n=N-1
or
N
).
We will refer to the ranges

\mathcal{F}_{N_{s}+1},\ldots\mathcal{F}_{N}
(or sometimes simply to the indices
n=N_{s}+1,\ldots,N
themselves)
as the
non-superradiant ranges
.

•

For
n=3,\ldots,N_{s}
, all
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}

are superradiant, i.e. satisfy (
3.16
),
and in fact
\omega/am
is uniformly bounded away from
0

and
\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}}
. We will refer to the ranges
\mathcal{F}_{3},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{N_{s}}
(or sometimes simply to the indices
n=3,\ldots,N_{s}
themselves) as
the
strictly superradiant ranges
.

•

Concerning
n=1
, since
0\in I_{1}
and
I_{1}
is open,
\mathcal{F}_{1}
consists of
both superradiant and nonsuperradiant

(\omega,m)
(such that
\omega/am\in I_{1}
), while
concerning
n=2
, since
\frac{1}{2Mr_{+}}\in I_{2}
and
I_{2}
is open, it follows that

\mathcal{F}_{2}
again contains
both superradiant and nonsuperradiant
(\omega,m)
(such that
\omega/am\in I_{2}
).
Similarly, concerning
n=0
,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
contains both superradiant and nonsuperradiant
frequencies. Nonetheless, in our arguments, we shall usually handle the low frequencies

\mathcal{F}_{0}
and the overlap cases
\mathcal{F}_{1}
,
\mathcal{F}_{2}

in a similar way as with the strictly superradiant frequencies. Thus we shall refer
collectively
to the ranges

\mathcal{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{N_{s}}

(or sometimes simply to the indices
n=0,\ldots,N_{s}
themselves) as the
generalised superradiant frequency ranges
.

•

To each non-zero generalised
superradiant range
n=1,\ldots,N_{s}
, then,
for all
admissible
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
with

(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, we have

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)=1

(3.41)

identically. Concerning the low frequency generalised superradiant range
n=0
, note that we have already established (
3.41
)
for all admissible
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
with
(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
; see (
3.40
).

•

Moreover, associated to each non-zero generalised superradiant range
n=1,\ldots,N_{s}
,
there exists a corresponding non-empty interval
[r_{n,1},r_{n,2}]\subset(r_{\rm pot},R_{\rm pot})

such that
[r_{n,1},r_{n,2}]\subset{\bf R}_{\rm elliptic}(\omega,m,\Lambda)
for
all admissible
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
with

(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
(We recall here that
r_{\rm pot}
,
R_{\rm pot}
are parameters fixed in Section
A.1.5
, which themselves
will satisfy
r_{1}<r_{\rm pot}<2.01M<5M<R_{\rm pot}<2R_{\rm pot}<R_{\rm freq}<R/2
.)
For the low frequency generalised superradiant range
n=0
, we may define for instance

r_{0,1}:=4M,\,r_{0,2}:=5M.

For all generalised superradiant ranges
n=0,\ldots,N_{s}
,
we now denote the analogous spacetime region by

\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\{r_{n,1}\leq r\leq r_{n,2}\}.

•

For the nonsuperradiant ranges
n=N_{s}+1,\ldots,N
, there again exists a corresponding
nonempty interval
[r_{n,1},r_{n,2}]\subset(r_{\rm pot},R_{\rm pot})
,
but now with the property that
for all
admissible
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
with

(\omega,m)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}

\chi_{\natural}(r,\omega,m,\Lambda)\gtrsim 1\qquad\text{for all\ }r\not\in(r_{n,1},r_{n,2}).

(3.42)

Let us denote the corresponding spacetime region by

\mathcal{D}_{n}:=\{r_{n,1}\leq r\leq r_{n,2}\}

and set
\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\emptyset
.
Moreover, associated to each
n=N_{s}+1,\ldots,N
,
there is an
\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

satisfying

0\leq\alpha_{n}\frac{2Mr_{+}}{a}\leq 1

(3.43)

such that the vector field
T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}

is future directed timelike on
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, in fact, such that

-g_{a,M}(T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1},T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1})\gtrsim 1.

(3.44)

(See (
A.50
).)
This implies that
T+\alpha_{n}\Omega
is in fact timelike in (and satisfies a similar bound in)
an enlarged region

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\{r^{\prime}_{n,1}\leq r\leq r^{\prime}_{n,2}\}

(3.45)

for some

r_{\rm pot}<r^{\prime}_{n,1}<r_{n,1}
,
R_{\rm pot}>r^{\prime}_{n,2}>r_{n,2}
.
which we now fix.

•

Returning to the generalised superradiant
ranges
n=0,\ldots,N_{s}
, let us set
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\emptyset
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}:=\emptyset

and set
\alpha_{n}:=\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}
. Thus, in this case
T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}

coincides with
Z
defined by (
2.3
), which we recall is a null generator of the Kerr horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Definition of
P_{n}
.

Now let
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\rho}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
n=1,\ldots,N
be a smooth partition of unity
of
\mathbb{R}
subordinate to the open cover
I_{n}
, and let
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be
a smooth function such that
\eta(z)=1
for
|z|\leq 1.1B_{\rm low}
,
\eta(z)=0
for
|z|\geq 1.9B_{\rm low}
. With this, define

\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{0}(\omega,m)=\eta(\omega^{2}+m^{2}),\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}(\omega,m)=(1-\eta(\omega^{2}+m^{2}))\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\rho}_{n}(\omega/am),\,\,n=1,\ldots,N

(3.46)

where if
m=0
,
\omega/a<0
, we define

\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{N-1}(\omega,m)=1-\eta(\omega^{2}+m^{2})

and
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}(\omega,m)=0
for all
n\neq 0,N-1
, while
while if
m=0
,
\omega/a\geq 0
, we define

\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{N}(\omega,m)=1-\eta(\omega^{2}+m^{2})
, while

\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}(\omega,m)=0
for all
n\neq 0,N
.
We will view these as functions
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
but they
are of course the restrictions of functions
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

In this sense, we see easily that (
3.46
) defines
a smooth partition of unity
\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}
,
n=0,\ldots,N
, of
(\omega,m)
frequency
space (interpreted for the moment as
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
)
subordinate to the open cover
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.

Define finally

P_{n}=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}\,\mathfrak{F}).

(3.47)

Note that

\sum_{n=0}^{N}P_{n}=Id.

(3.48)

Let us note that in the region
r>r_{+}
, the
P_{n}
may also be expressed
as
P_{n}=\mathfrak{F}_{BL}^{-1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.45}{\mbox{\tiny\text{\Acht}}}}{\chi}_{n}\,\mathfrak{F}_{BL})
.

Let us set

\psi_{n,{\bf k}}=P_{n}(\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi)

(3.49)

where
\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}
is the cutoff of Section
3.2.1

and
\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}
are the entirely Killing commutation operators
of (
2.4
).
(Let us note that
[P_{n},\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}]=0
, and thus we may rewrite
this
as

\psi_{n,{\bf k}}=\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}P_{n}(\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi)=\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\psi_{n}

in the notation (
1.39
) of the introduction.)

Properties of
P_{n}
.

Because the operators
P_{n}
above
are defined with smooth cutoffs in both physical and frequency space with
nice homogeneity properties, we have the following:

Proposition 3.4.1
.

The operators
P_{n}
,
P_{n}\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}
are zeroth order
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
-pseudodifferential operators
in the sense of Section
3.2.2
.

Proof.

This is elementary given the way
P_{n}
is defined and the homogeneity properties
of (
3.46
).
∎

Let us already state an improved physical space bound that applies
to functions projected by
P_{n}
.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Let
\psi
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

Define
\psi_{n,{\bf k}}
by (
3.49
), for
{\bf k}
with
{|\bf k}|=k-1
.
Then we have

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n}}r^{-2}\left(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}\right)\lesssim{}^{\natural}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi]+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi].

More generally,
consider a
(t^{*},\phi^{*})
pseudodifferential operator of order
k-1

whose symbol
q_{\rm symb}
is supported in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and in
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Then

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n}}r^{-2}\left(|LQ\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}\right)\lesssim{}^{\natural}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi]+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi].

Proof.

This follows from Plancherel and the lower bounds (
3.41
)
and (
3.42
).
∎

Remark 3.4.3
.

Concerning the
r^{-2}
factor in the integrands above, see Remark
3.3.1
.

We will need one final proposition which may be proven using the
pseudodifferential calculus:

Proposition 3.4.4
.

Let
P_{n}
be the projection (
3.47
) and let
\mu
be a smooth
function on
\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}
.
Then

[P_{n},\mu]=Q_{1}+Q_{2}

where
Q_{1}
is of order
-1
and the support of its symbol
in frequency space is contained in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, while

Q_{2}
is of order
-2
.

3.4.2
The currents
J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}
and boundary coercivity

We now introduce the fundamental new ingredient of the present
paper replacing 2. of
[
DHRT22
]
: a set
of translation invariant currents
J^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
,

n=0,\ldots,N
.

For each
n
, the corresponding current
may be expressed in the form
J^{V_{n},w_{n},q_{n},\varpi_{n}}_{g_{a,M}}
from (
2.9
)
for suitable choices of these functions.
It will be more natural, however,
to realise this current as a sum of “twisted currents” with
twisting function
(r^{2}+a^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
This is reviewed in Appendix
A.2
,
and, in the notation introduced there, the
current has the following form in the region
r\geq r_{+}
for non-superradiant frequencies
n
:

\displaystyle J^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tilde{J}^{y_{n}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{f_{n}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{f_{\rm fixed}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{y_{\rm fixed}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{\hat{z}}[\Psi]+e_{\rm red}\tilde{J}^{z}_{\rm red}[\Psi]

(3.50)

\displaystyle\qquad+E\left(\tilde{J}^{T}[\Psi]+\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}\frac{(2Mr_{+}\alpha_{n}/a)}{(1-2Mr_{+}\alpha_{n}/a)}\tilde{J}^{Z}[\Psi])\right)

while for generalised superradiant frequencies
n

it has the form:

\displaystyle J^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\tilde{J}^{y_{n}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{f_{n}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{f_{\rm fixed}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{y_{\rm fixed}}[\Psi]+\tilde{J}^{\hat{z}}[\Psi]+e_{\rm red}\tilde{J}^{z}_{\rm red}[\Psi]

(3.51)

\displaystyle\qquad+E(\tilde{J}^{T}[\Psi]+\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}\alpha_{n}\tilde{J}^{\Omega_{1}}[\Psi]).

(We have dropped
g_{a,M}
subscripts from the currents on the right hand side.)
Here
E>0
and
e_{\rm red}>0
are large and small positive parameters, respectively,
which must be chosen appropriately, and
\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}
is a cutoff which depends
on the parameter
R_{\rm freq}
, which also must be chosen appropriately.
We will define the corresponding bulk currents as
\tilde{K}^{y_{n}}
, etc.,
and the total bulk current by
K^{{\rm main},n}
.

For the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}
, the exact form of the currents (
3.50
)–(
3.51
) is in fact
not important because
we shall be able to deduce the relevant properties
via continuity from the behaviour at
r=r_{+}
, after restriction of
r_{0}
(see for instance already Proposition
3.4.6
).
Thus, we may define
J^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]
in the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}
to be in fact arbitrary smooth
extensions of the above. (Since some of the component currents of (
3.50
)–(
3.51
) (like the Killing currents) have natural extensions to
r\leq r_{+}
, one may think of this as equivalent to choosing smooth
extensions of the
y_{n}
and
f_{n}
currents.)

We record only those properties which will be already necessary for the discussion of the
statements
of the propositions of the present section as well as those of Section
3.4.3

immediately below. All properties referred to below follow easily from
the basic support properties of the definitions of the functions
y_{n}
,
f_{n}
,
f_{\rm fixed}
, etc.
as described in
Section
A.4
, to which (along with Section
A.5
) the proofs of some of the propositions stated in this and the next section
are also deferred:

•

The current components
\tilde{J}^{f_{\rm fixed}}
,
\tilde{J}^{\hat{z}}
are
n
-independent and supported only in

r\geq R_{\rm pot}

(thus similarly for
\tilde{K}^{f_{\rm fixed}}
,
\tilde{K}^{\hat{z}}
)
while
\tilde{J}^{z}_{\rm red}
is again
n
-independent and supported only in
r\leq r_{2}

(thus similarly for
\tilde{K}^{z}_{\rm red}
). (See Section
A.4.1
.)

•

\tilde{J}^{T}_{a,M}
,
\tilde{J}_{a,M}^{\Omega_{1}}

are the twisted energy currents corresponding to vector fields

T
and
\Omega_{1}
defined by expressions (
A.69
), (
A.69
), respectively,
and
Z=T+\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}\Omega_{1}
,
\tilde{J}_{a,M}^{Z}=\tilde{J}^{T}_{a,M}+\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}\tilde{J}_{a,M}^{\Omega_{1}}
.

•

\tilde{J}^{z}_{\rm red}
is in fact the twisted energy current
\tilde{J}_{a,M}^{X}

of a vector field
X
such that, given arbitrary
e_{\rm red}>0
,

e_{\rm red}X+EZ
is timelike on
r=r_{0}
for
E>0
sufficiently large
and
r_{0}
sufficiently close to
r_{+}
. See (
A.68
).

•

In the generalised superradiant case,

\chi^{\prime}_{{\rm Killing},n}
will be supported
in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
,
\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}=1
near
r=r_{0}
and
\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}=0
for large
r
(see (
A.110
))
and
\tilde{J}^{y_{n}}
will vanish at some value of
r
in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
(see (
A.111
)).
Recall that in this case,
\alpha_{n}=\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}
.

•

In the non-superradiant case,
\chi^{\prime}_{{\rm Killing},n}

is supported in
[R_{\rm pot},R_{\rm freq}]
,
\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}=1
near
r=r_{0}
and
\chi_{{\rm Killing},n}=0
for large
r
(see (
A.105
))
and
{\tilde{J}}^{y_{n}}
,

{\tilde{J}}^{f_{n}}

(and thus
\tilde{K}^{y_{n}}
,
\tilde{K}^{f_{n}}
) will vanish identically in
\mathcal{D}_{n}

(see (
A.97
), (
A.102
)).

•

The currents

\tilde{J}^{y_{n}}[\Psi]
are
n
-independent for
r\geq 1.2R_{\rm pot}

and the currents
\tilde{J}^{f_{n}}[\Psi]
vanish for
r\geq R_{\rm pot}
.

It follows in particular from the above that the total current (
3.50
), (
3.51
) is in fact

n
-independent for
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
,
where we may write

J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main}}[\Psi]:=J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}[\Psi],\qquad K_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main}}[\Psi]:=K_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}[\Psi].

Using the properties listed above alone, we can deduce the following boundary coercivity statement in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
for the
currents corresponding to non-superradiant ranges
n
:

Proposition 3.4.5

(Boundary coercivity in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
for non-superradiant
ranges)
.

Let
E>0
be sufficiently large in the definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
).
For a general function
\Psi
and all
0\leq n\leq N
, we have that in
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\Psi]\cdot{\rm n}\gtrsim|L\Psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2}+|\Psi|^{2},

(3.52)

where
{\rm n}={\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}
.

Proof.

(Recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}=\emptyset
in the generalised superradiant case, so the proposition is only nontrivial in the case of non-superradiant
n
, where

\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
is the enlarged region defined by (
3.45
).)

Restricted to
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, the statement follows because by the above discussion,
the current reduces to

E(1-2Mr_{+}\alpha_{n}/a)^{-1}(\tilde{J}^{T}[\Psi]+\alpha_{n}\tilde{J}^{\Omega_{1}}[\Psi])=E\tilde{J}^{T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}}[\Psi].

Thus the boundary term integrand on
\mathcal{D}_{n}\cap\Sigma(\tau)
may be expressed

E(1-2Mr_{+}\alpha_{n}/a)^{-1}\tilde{T}(T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1},n_{\Sigma(\tau)})

where
\tilde{T}
is the twisted energy momentum tensor (
A.66
),
and this quantity is coercive since
E>0
and
T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1}
and
n_{\Sigma(\tau)}

are timelike. Thus, in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, the bound (
3.52
) would
follow for any
E>0
.

More generally, given
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
as defined,
where
-g(T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1},T+\alpha_{n}\Omega_{1})\geq b
, then
if
E
is sufficiently large we may absorb all other terms and again obtain (
3.52
).
∎

For currents corresponding to generalised superradiant ranges, on the other hand, we have the following coercivity statement at
\mathcal{S}
:

Proposition 3.4.6

(Boundary coercivity at
\mathcal{S}
for generalised superradiant currents)
.

Let
e_{\rm red}>0
be arbitrary. Let
E>0
be sufficiently large in the definition (
3.51
) and
r_{0}<r_{+}
be sufficiently close to
r_{+}
, both depending on
e_{\rm red}
.
For a general function
\Psi
, and for generalised superradiant
n
, we have on
\mathcal{S}

the pointwise relation:

{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\Psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq c(r_{0})(|L\Psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2})-C|\Psi|^{2},

(3.53)

Proof.

The statement follows immediately from the definition
\alpha_{n}=\frac{a}{2Mr_{+}}
in the
generalised superradiant
case, in view of the presence also of
e_{\rm red}\tilde{J}^{z}_{\rm red}
and the
fact recorded earlier that
e_{\rm red}X+EZ
is timelike on
r=r_{0}
for
r_{0}
sufficiently
close to
r_{+}
and
E>0
sufficiently large, and thus
\tilde{J}^{e_{\rm red}X+EZ}
is coercive
and in fact can absorb all other boundary terms. (That it can absorb the remaining boundary terms can be seen easily from their structure at
r=r_{+}
and continuity; cf. also the proof of Proposition
3.4.8
below (in Section
A.5
)
where this is done in detail in the more complicated
non-superradiant case. Thus, in particular, this property is independent of the precise form of
the smooth extension to
r_{0}\leq r<r_{+}
.)
We note that until
e_{\rm red}
is fixed, the constant
c
above also depends on
e_{\rm red}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.7
.

We recall that according to the conventions of Section
2.1.5
, all
generic constants, like
c
in (
3.53
), may depend in principle
on all parameters not yet fixed. We have chosen to explicitly record the
r_{0}
-dependence
in (
3.53
)
for emphasis, as
c(r_{0})
indeed degenerates as
r_{0}\to r_{+}
.

In the case of non-superradiant
n
, the current
J^{{\rm main},n}
will not
be
pointwise
coercive at
\mathcal{S}
. It will however enjoy
integrated
coercivity
properties
when specialised to the image of
P_{n}
, stemming precisely from
the non-superradiant property of
P_{n}\psi
.
We first state the following global integrated coercivity property:

Proposition 3.4.8

(Global boundary coercivity on the image of
P_{n}
for non-superradiant
frequency ranges)
.

Let
e_{\rm red}>0
be arbitrary. Let
E>0
be sufficiently large
in the definition (
3.50
)
and
r_{0}<r_{+}
be sufficiently close to
r_{+}
, both depending on
e_{\rm red}
.

Let
\psi
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

and define
\psi_{n,{\bf k}}
by (
3.49
).
For non-superradiant
n
, then denoting
k=|{\bf k}|

we have

\int_{\mathcal{S}}{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq c(r_{0})\int_{\mathcal{S}}(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})-C\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi].

(3.54)

Proof.

See Section
A.5
.
∎

We may now localise the above using the pseudodifferential calculus.
For this, it will be important to introduce an additional parameter

0<\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}<\frac{1}{4}

(3.55)

which will be fixed later (see already Section
4.5
),
and, given
\tau_{0}+1\leq\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tilde{\tau}_{1}-1\leq\tilde{\tau}\leq\tau_{1}-1
, an associated cutoff

\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}
such that
\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}=1

in
\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}=0
in

\tau\leq\tilde{\tau}_{0}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
and
\tau\geq\tilde{\tau}_{1}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.
Note already the unfavourable
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
-dependence
|\partial_{t^{*}}\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}|\lesssim\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}^{-1}
, etc.
Let us also recall the
\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}

notation from (
2.21
).
We have the following localised version of Proposition
3.4.8
:

Proposition 3.4.9

(Localised boundary coercivity on the image of
P_{n}
for non-superradiant
frequency ranges)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
3.4.8
,
the following is moreover true:

For non-superradiant
n
and for the corresponding
\psi_{n,{\bf k}}
, then denoting

k=|{\bf k}|
we have
for all
\tau_{0}+1\leq\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tilde{\tau}_{1}-1\leq\tilde{\tau}\leq\tau_{1}-1

the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq

\displaystyle c(r_{0})\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})

(3.56)

\displaystyle\,-C\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{0}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff})[\psi]-C\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tilde{\tau}_{1}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{1}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff})[\psi]

(3.57)

\displaystyle\,-C\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],

(3.58)

where the constants in both lines (
3.56
) and (
3.57
) (but not (
3.58
)!), may be chosen independently
of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.

Remark 3.4.10
.

We may of course already replace
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
above
by
{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

in view of the definition (
3.34
). We note, however, that the
terms on line (
3.57
) will be top order error terms
in our later argument, and thus it will be essential that we can exploit the smallness
of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
. (See already (
4.64
). Note how it is important
that the constants of (
3.56
) and (
3.57
)
may be chosen independently of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.)

Proof.

We may apply (
3.54
) to

\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}:=P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}\psi,

(3.59)

where
\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}
is the
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
-dependent
cutoff defined above immediately following (
3.55
),
to obtain

\int_{\mathcal{S}}{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}]{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\int_{\mathcal{S}}c(r_{0})(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})-C\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

where we note that
c(r_{0})
may be chosen independently of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
(but not
C
!).
Thus, to obtain (
3.56
), it suffices to bound

\left|\int_{\mathcal{S}}{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}-\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}{J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\right|

(3.60)

and

\left|\int_{\mathcal{S}}(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})-\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}|+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})\right|

(3.61)

in terms
of a small multiple times

\int_{\mathcal{S}}(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})+\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}|+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}),

(3.62)

and the error terms on lines (
3.57
)
and (
3.58
).

The term (
3.61
) can be estimated by the sum of

\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}||L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}+L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}||\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}+\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}||\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}+\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|)

(3.63)

and

\int_{\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{1},\tilde{\tau}_{2})}|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}.

(3.64)

To estimate (
3.63
), by Cauchy–Schwarz, we may indeed estimate the one factor
by a small multiple times (
3.62
). It thus suffices to bound

\int_{\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}-\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}).

(3.65)

This may be bounded by

\|Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{S})}

where
Q
is the pseudodifferential operator

\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}(\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}-\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}})

and we may rewrite

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{1}+Q_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}(\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}-\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}})+[P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k},\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}](\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}-\chi^{2}_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}).

Now we have from Proposition
3.2.2
that

\|Q_{1}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{S})}\lesssim\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{0}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff})+\mathring{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tilde{\tau}_{1}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{1}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff})+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

(3.66)

where the implicit constant on the first two terms on the right hand side (but not the third!) may be chosen
independently of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}

while

\|Q_{2}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{S})}\lesssim\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

where we have used Proposition
3.2.4
and we recall
the energy of (
2.21
).

To estimate (
3.64
) on the other hand, we note that this
may be estimated by

\|Q\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{S})}

where
Q=(1-\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{1}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}})P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\chi_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}

and thus this term may again be estimated by the right hand side of (
3.66
).

We have estimated (
3.61
) as desired. To estimate (
3.60
),
we note
that
J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}[\Psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a quadratic expression in
\Psi
and derivatives
of
\Psi
. We may thus bound (
3.60
) as a sum of terms analogous to (
3.63
) and (
3.64
)
and argue exactly as above.

By our above remarks, the proposition follows.
∎

3.4.3
The degeneration functions
\chi_{n}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{n}
and the generalised bulk coercivity property of
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
in
r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}

on the image of
P_{n}

We now turn to the issue of (degenerate) bulk coercivity in
r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}
.

We can already deduce of course
from our definitions above that for a general function
\Psi
,

K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]=0

in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
For general
\Psi
, however, we will not have good bulk properties
in
\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
In this section, we will show that
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
has nonnegativity properties
(and in fact suitable
degenerate
coercivity properties), modulo error terms,

when restricted to the image of the associated projection
P_{n}
.

As announced already at the beginning of Section
3.4
, the results
of the present section will in particular
require frequency analysis based on Carter’s full separation, in order
to correctly exploit the allowed error terms. Thus, the proof of Theorem
3.3
below
will only be given in Appendix
A
, to which all uses of Carter’s separation are deferred.

The spacetime degeneration of the coercive control provided by our
bulk current
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
will be captured by a function
\chi_{n}(r)
.
We define
\chi_{n}

to be a nonnegative function
with the following properties:
For the generalised superradiant frequency ranges
n
, we simply set
\chi_{n}=1
identically.
For the nonsuperradiant frequency ranges
n
,
we define
\chi_{n}
to be suitably regular so that

•

\chi_{n}=0
in a set whose interior contains
[r_{n,1},r_{n,2}]
,

•

\chi_{n}=1
for
r\leq r^{\prime}_{n,1}
and
r\geq r_{n,2}^{\prime}
, where
we recall these parameters from (
3.45
),

•

|\chi_{n}^{\prime}|\lesssim\sqrt{\chi}_{n}
.

We define finally an auxiliary nonnegative function

\tilde{\chi}_{n}
to be such that
\tilde{\chi}_{n}=0
in
(r_{n,1},r_{n,2})
but

\tilde{\chi}_{n}=1
in the support of
\chi_{n}
. (Again, for generalised superradiant

n
, we define
\tilde{\chi}_{n}=1
identically.)
The function
\chi_{n}
will appear in the definition of
f_{n}
and
y_{n}
appearing
in the
currents (
3.50
).

We may now state the generalised bulk coercivity statement which is at the
heart of our argument:

Theorem 3.3

(Global generalised bulk coercivity on the image of
P_{n}
)
.

We may choose
b_{\rm trap}>0
,
\gamma_{\rm elliptic}>0

and
b_{\rm elliptic}>0
sufficiently small in (
3.28
), (
3.30
)
and (
3.31
),
respectively, so that
the following holds.

Let
E>0
be arbitrary and let
e_{\rm red}>0
be sufficiently small in the definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
). Then we may choose
R_{\rm freq}

and all functions appearing in these definitions so that the following is true.

Let
\psi
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

and, for
0\leq n\leq N
, define
\psi_{n,{\bf k}}
by (
3.49
). Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c\int_{\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}\right)

(3.67)

\displaystyle\quad-C\,{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]-C\,{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]-C\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi].

Note in particular the presence of
{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]
on the right hand side.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.4
.
∎

We may now localise the above to appropriate
subregions
\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{1},\tilde{\tau}_{2})\subset\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
using the pseudodifferential calculus:

Proposition 3.4.11

(Localised generalised bulk coercivity on the image of
P_{n}
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.3
, the following
is moreover true:

For
all
\tau_{0}+1\leq\tilde{\tau}_{0}\leq\tilde{\tau}_{1}-1\leq\tilde{\tau}_{1}\leq\tau_{1}-1

we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}}K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}\right)

(3.68)

\displaystyle\qquad-C\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}\,\sup_{\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)[\psi]-C\,{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

where
k:=|{\bf k}|
, where the constant
c
and the constant
C
multiplying
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
may
be chosen independently of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.

Remark 3.4.12
.

Note that the statement of the proposition is indeed compatible with the conventions on the dependence of generic constants on
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}

from Section
2.1.5
.
In contrast, the constant multiplying

{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]
will depend unfavourably on
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.
Again we remark that the first term on line (
3.68
) will be a top order error term
in our later argument, and we must thus exploit the smallness of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.
See already the proof of Proposition
4.5.1
.

Proof.

In view of (
3.67
) shown in
Theorem
3.3
, given
\tilde{\tau}_{1}\leq\tilde{\tau}_{2}-2
,
we define
again

\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}
by (
3.59
) with the

\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
-dependent cutoff
\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}
,
and we have that (
3.67
) holds replacing

\psi_{n,{\bf k}}
with
\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}
and
\tau_{0}
,
\tau_{1}
with

\tilde{\tau}_{0}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{1}
, and where the constant
c
(but not the constants

C
!) may be chosen independently of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.

Now note that by (
3.36
),
we have

{}^{\rm elp}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})+{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\lesssim{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

where we remark however that the implicit constant has unfavourable dependence on
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.

To prove (
3.68
)
from (
3.67
) applied to
\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}
, we must estimate the
following terms:

\int_{\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left|\,\iota_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}(|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})-(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2})\,\right|\,,

(3.69)

\int_{\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\left|\,\iota_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})}K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi_{n,{\bf k}}]-K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}]\,\right|.

(3.70)

We estimate first (
3.69
).
We may clearly estimate this by the sum of

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left(\,|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|\,|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}+{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|\,|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}+{\underline{L}}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|\,|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}+\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}|\,\right)\,

(3.71)

and

\int_{(\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}))\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}(|L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}).

(3.72)

To estimate (
3.71
), we will apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the products putting a small constant on the second
factors. The quadratic term arising from the second factors can be absorbed then by the main terms we are comparing,
as in the proof of Proposition
3.4.9
.
Thus, to estimate (
3.71
), it suffices to bound the quadratic term arising
from the first factors, namely:

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\chi_{n}r^{-2}\left(\,|L\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-L\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-\underline{L}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi_{n,{\bf k}}-\not{\nabla}\widetilde{\psi}_{n,{\bf k}}|^{2}\right)\,.

(3.73)

We may bound (
3.73
) by

\eqref{againareduction}\lesssim\|Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n})},

where

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}(P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}-P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}(\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}-\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}(\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}-\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}})+[\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}},P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}](\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}-\tilde{\chi}^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q_{1}+Q_{2}(\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}-\chi^{2}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}),

where
Q_{1}
is a pseudodifferential operator of order
k
and
Q_{2}
is a
pseudodifferential operator of order
k-1
.

Examining the precise form of
Q_{1}
, we see from Proposition
3.2.2
that

\|Q_{1}\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n})}\lesssim\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)+{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}[\psi],

where the implicit constant for the first term (but not the second!) may be chosen
independent of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.
On the other hand, using Proposition
3.4.4
we may rewrite

Q_{2}=Q_{3}+Q_{4}

where
Q_{3}
is of order
k-1

supported in frequency space entirely in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
,
while
Q_{4}
is of order
k-2
. It follows from Proposition
3.4.2
that

\|Q_{3}\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n})}\lesssim{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}[\psi]+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi]

while

\|Q_{4}\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\})}\lesssim{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi].

(Here all constants have unfavourable dependence on
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.)

We may estimate thus (
3.73
) by

\eqref{againareduction}\lesssim\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)+{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}[\psi]+{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}[\psi],

(3.74)

where the implicit constant multiplying
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}\sup_{\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{1}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{E}}}(\tau)
may be taken independent of
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
.
By our above remarks, this allows us to estimate (
3.71
) by (
3.74
)
and terms that we may absorb into the bulk integral on the right hand side of (
3.68
).

To estimate (
3.72
) on the other hand,
we remark that this may be estimated

\eqref{bythesumofthisalso}\lesssim\|Q\chi_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}}\psi\|^{2}_{H^{0,1}(\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm freq}\}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{n})},

where

Q=(1-\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0}+\epsilon_{\rm cutoff},\tilde{\tau}_{1}-\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}})P_{n}\mathring{\mathfrak{D}}^{\bf k}\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1}},

and this may again be estimated by the right hand side of (
3.74
)
by decomposing
Q
appropriately as above.

Since we have estimated both (
3.73
) and (
3.72
),
we have thus indeed estimated (
3.69
) in the desired manner.

To obtain the proposition, it remains then to estimate (
3.70
).
We argue similarly.
Let us first note the following general bound, which uses some more information
about our current:

Lemma 3.4.13

(General boundedness properties of
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
)
.

For a general function
\psi
, we have

\left|\,K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi]\,\right|\lesssim(\chi_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}r^{-1}\left(|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}\right)+\tilde{\chi}_{n}r^{-3}|\psi|^{2}

(3.75)

where
\chi_{n}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{n}
are as defined
above. (In particular the right hand side of (
3.75
)
vanishes identically in
\mathcal{D}_{n}
.)
In fact the stronger statement holds that all terms on the right hand side
of (
2.10
) can be individually bounded
in norm by the right hand side of (
3.75
).

Proof of Lemma
3.4.13
.

This is clear from the form of the definition of the currents (
3.50
)–(
3.51
) in Section
A.4
and the definition of
\chi_{n}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{n}
. Note that when expanding these currents
in the form (
2.10
) there will be pure first order terms, mixed terms and
zeroth order terms. The first order terms can be bounded by the first term on the
right hand side of (
3.75
)
while the zeroth order terms can be bounded by the second term.
The mixed terms can be bounded by the sum of the two terms using
Cauchy–Schwarz (in fact with
\chi_{n}

replacing
(\chi_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}
).
∎

Note that
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi]
is a sum of quadratic terms, including
purely first order terms, mixed terms and zeroth order terms and the above
lemma bounds all of these terms individually in norm.

The contribution of all zeroth order terms in (
3.70
)
can be bounded by sums of terms of the form

\int_{\mathcal{M}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R_{\rm fixed}\}}\tilde{\chi}_{n}\left(|LQ\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}Q\chi_{\tau_{0},\tau_{1}}\psi|^{2}\right)

(3.76)

where
Q
is a pseudodifferential operator of order
k-1
whose symbol has
frequency support contained in the support of
P_{n}
.
Thus, Proposition
3.4.2
applies to bound this term by

{}^{\natural}\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
which is of course
in turn estimated by
{}^{\chi\natural}_{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny{\boxed{+1}}}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
appearing on the right
hand side of (
3.68
).

The contribution of all mixed terms in (
3.70
)
can be handled by applying Cauchy–Schwarz, throwing a small constant
on the resulting first order terms and a large constant on the zeroth order terms,
and as in the proof of Lemma
3.4.13
, noting that one can replace
(\chi_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}

with
\chi_{n}
.
The zeroth order terms are bounded again by (
3.76
), while the first order terms,
for a sufficiently small constant, in view of the
\chi_{n}
can be absorbed in
the main term being estimated.

Thus, it suffices to estimate the difference of purely first order quadratic terms
contained in (
3.70
).
Using Lemma
3.4.13
, we may bound
the difference of the purely first order quadratic terms by the sum of
(
3.71
) and (
3.72
),
where
(\chi_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}
is replaced by
\chi_{n}
.
The arguments above still apply then
and these terms are again estimated by (
3.74
)
and a term which can be absorbed in the bulk integral on the right hand side of (
3.68
).
This yields the desired estimate on (
3.70
), and thus completes the proof.
∎

3.4.4
Bulk coercivity properties of
K_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main},n}
in the regions
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}
and

r\geq R_{\rm freq}

Concerning the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}
within the Kerr event horizon, we may essentially infer bulk coercivity
softly from the statements already shown, after restriction of
r_{0}
:

Proposition 3.4.14

(Bulk coercivity of
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
in
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.3
,
after choosing
r_{0}
sufficiently close to
r_{+}
, then
for a general function
\Psi
and all
0\leq n\leq N
, we have in the region
r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{+}

the coercivity statement

K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]\geq c(|L\Psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2})-C|\Psi|^{2}.

Proof.

After choosing
r_{0}
sufficiently close to
r_{+}
,
this follows softly from the analogous statement in
r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{\rm close}
shown in the
proof of Theorem
3.3
in Appendix
A.4
, independently
of the precise extension of the currents to
r<r_{+}
.
∎

Recall that
K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}
is independent of
n

in the region
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
, and we write

K^{{\rm main},n}_{g_{a,M}}=K^{{\rm main}}_{g_{a,M}}
.
We have the following bulk coercivity statement:

Proposition 3.4.15

(Bulk coercivity of
K^{{\rm main}}_{g_{a,M}}
in
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.3
,
for a general function
\Psi
, then in the region
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
, we have
the coercivity statement

K^{{\rm main}}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]\gtrsim r^{-3}(|L\Psi|)^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2}+r^{-1}|\Psi|^{2}).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
A.4.1
of Appendix
A.4
.
∎

3.5
The currents
J{}_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm redder}
,
J{}_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm total}
and improved coercivity properties

Given
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
or
p=0
, we define
in the region
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
the current

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]:=J_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm main}}[\psi]+e_{\rm total}\zeta\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}^{\rm far}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi],

where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}^{\rm far}
is the current defined in Appendix B of
[
DHRT22
]
,

\zeta
is the cutoff function of Section
2.1.3

and
e_{\rm total}>0
is a sufficiently small
parameter.

We also define the current

J_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm redder}[\psi]:=\hat{\hat{\zeta}}J^{\varsigma(k)}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi],

where
J^{\varsigma(k)}{}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi]
is the current from
Section 3.5 of
[
DHRT22
]
and

\hat{\hat{\zeta}}
is a cutoff function
such that
\hat{\hat{\zeta}}(r)=1
for
r\leq r_{1}+2\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{3}

and
\hat{\hat{\zeta}}(r)=0
for
r\geq r_{2}

(cf. the cutoff function
\hat{\zeta}

of Section
2.1.3
).

Recall that these currents have improved
coercivity properties, allowing us in particular
to absorb top order commutation errors generated by
\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi

for
k_{i}\neq 0
for
i\geq 2
.

We first consider
J^{\rm redder}_{g_{a,M}}
and the region near the horizon.
Concerning the boundary, we have

Proposition 3.5.1

(Boundary coercivity of
J^{\rm redder}
on
\mathcal{S}
and nonnegativity
on
\Sigma_{\tau}
)
.

We have

J_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm redder}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq c(r_{0})(|L\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}),

and

J_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm redder}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}\geq 0.

(3.77)

Proof.

This is of course immediate from Proposition 3.4.2 of
[
DHRT22
]
.
We have in fact a stronger coercivity statement in place of (
4.29
)
for
r\leq r_{1}
say, but we shall not make direct use of that. We note that (
3.77
) in fact
vanishes for
r\geq r_{2}
.
∎

Concerning the bulk, we have

Proposition 3.5.2

(Improved bulk coercivity of
K^{\rm redder}
near
\mathcal{S}
)
.

For all
r_{2}>r_{1}>r_{+}
sufficiently close to
r_{+}
,
we have for all functions
\Psi
the coercivity

K{}^{{\rm redder}}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]\gtrsim|L\Psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2}\qquad\text{\ in\ }r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}+2\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{3}

and for any
k\geq 1
, there exists a positive real weight function
\alpha({\bf k})
defined
on
|{\bf k}|=k

such that, for all solutions
\psi

of the homogeneous
\Box_{g}\psi=0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{|{\bf k}|=k}\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}+2\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{3}\}}K{}^{{\rm redder}}_{g_{a,M}}[\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi]+{\rm Re}(H{}^{\rm redder}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi]\cdot\overline{\Box_{g_{a,M}}\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}+2\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{3}\}}\frac{1}{3}K{}^{\rm redder}_{g_{a,M}}[\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi]-C\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})[\psi].

(3.78)

Proof.

This is immediate from Proposition 3.4.2 of
[
DHRT22
]
and
the elliptic estimates of Proposition
2.3.3
. (We note that the second term
in the integral on the left hand side is supported only in the region
r\leq r_{1}+\frac{r_{2}-r_{1}}{2}

in view of the definition of the cutoff
\hat{\zeta}
in Section
2.1.3
.)
See the proof of Proposition 3.6.4 of
[
DHRT22
]
for the more general inhomogeneous case.
∎

We now turn to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm total}
. Concerning the boundary, we have

Proposition 3.5.3

(Improved boundary coercivity in
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
)
.

Let
E>0
be sufficiently large in the definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
). For a general function
\psi
, we have for
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
, and
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
the boundary coercivity

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}\gtrsim r^{p}|r^{-1}L(r\psi)|^{2}+r^{\frac{p}{2}}|L\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+r^{\frac{p}{2}-2}|\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}

while for
p=0
we have

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\Sigma(\tau)}\gtrsim|L\psi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+r^{-2}|\psi|^{2}+\iota_{r\leq R}|\underline{L}\psi|^{2}.

Proof.

This follows from Sections B.1 and B.2 of
[
DHRT22
]
.
∎

Concerning the bulk, we have

Proposition 3.5.4

(Improved bulk coercivity in
r\geq R_{\rm freq}
)
.

Let
E>0
be sufficiently large in the definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
).
For
e_{\rm total}>0
sufficiently small, we have for
r\geq R_{\rm freq}

and all functions
\Psi
the coercivity statements

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{K}{}^{{\rm total}}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle\gtrsim r^{p-1}((r^{-1}|Lr\Psi|)^{2}+|L\Psi|+|\not{\nabla}\Psi|^{2})+r^{-1-\delta}|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2}+r^{p-3}|\Psi|^{2}\qquad\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta,

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{K}{}^{{\rm total}}_{g_{a,M}}[\Psi]

\displaystyle\gtrsim r^{-1-\delta}(|L\Psi|+|\not{\nabla}\psi|^{2}+|\underline{L}\Psi|^{2})+r^{-3-\delta}|\Psi|^{2}\qquad p=0,

and, for any
k\geq 1
,
the
\alpha({\bf k})
of Proposition
3.5.2
may be chosen so that
moreover,
for all solutions of the homogeneous equation
\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi=0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r\geq R/4\}}\sum_{|{\bf k}|=k}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{K}{}^{{\rm total}}_{g_{a,M}}[\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi]+{\rm Re}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{H}{}^{{\rm total}}_{g_{a,M}}[\psi]\cdot\overline{\Box_{g_{a,M}}\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})\cap\{r\geq R/4\}}\frac{1}{3}\sum_{|{\bf k}|=k}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{K}{}^{{\rm total}}_{g_{a,M}}[\alpha({\bf k})\mathfrak{D}^{\bf k}\psi]-C\,{}^{\chi}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k\mkern-6.0mu-\mkern-6.0mu1$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}(\tilde{\tau}_{0},\tilde{\tau}_{1})[\psi].

(3.79)

Proof.

This follows from Section 3.6.6 of
[
DHRT22
]
, where the more general
inhomogeneous version is obtained. (We recall that in a region
r\geq R_{k}
(depending on
k
, however, with
R_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
!), (
3.79
) follows in fact from a pointwise coercivity bound, while
for the region
R/2\leq r\leq R_{k}
one must
exploit the elliptic estimate (
2.38
).)
∎

Proposition 3.5.5

(Boundary coercivity at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
)
.

Let
E>0
be sufficiently large in the definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
).
For a general function
\psi
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,
suppose

\int_{\Sigma(\tau_{0})\cap\{r\geq R\}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}<\infty,\qquad\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\geq R\}}|\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{H}^{\rm total}[\psi]\Box_{g_{a,M}}\psi|<\infty.

(3.80)

Then we may integrate the divergence identity of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{\rm total}[\psi]
globally
in
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\geq R\}
with a well defined additional boundary term
at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
which satisfies the coercivity property:

\int_{\mathcal{I}^{+}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}\geq 0.

(3.81)

Proof.

This follows from a straightforward limiting argument from the energy identity (
2.13
) on a compact region. The left hand side of (
3.81
)
may be interpreted as
\lim_{v\to\infty}\int_{C_{v}\cap\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{J}{}_{g_{a,M}}^{{\rm total}}[\psi]\cdot{\rm n}_{C_{v}}
, whose coercivity follows from
Section B.2 of
[
DHRT22
]
.
∎

We will only use Proposition
3.5.5
to throw away the boundary term (
3.81
)
in the energy identity.
See already Proposition
4.5.1
.

3.6
Fixing the parameters

Proposition
3.5.5
, which constrains
E
, provides in fact the final constraint on our parameters. We now choose
e_{\rm red}>0
sufficiently small to satisfy all the smallness
assumptions and

E>0
sufficiently large so as to satisfy all largeness assumptions of the
various propositions of this section. We fix then

R_{\rm freq}
from Theorem
3.3
,
e_{\rm total}
from Proposition
3.5.4
, and
r_{0}<r_{+}

sufficiently close to
r_{+}
satisfying the closeness requirements of the propositions
of this section.

We fix
r_{1}
so as to satisfy the closeness of Proposition
3.5.2

as well as the constraints

r_{1}<r_{\rm pot}
where
r_{\rm pot}
is a parameter appearing in Appendix
A
(fixed at the beginning of Section
A.1.5
)
and we fix now also
r_{+}<r_{1}<r_{2}<r_{\rm pot}
.

We fix finally
R
such that
R/4\geq R_{\rm freq}
.

We recall that the parameters

b_{\rm trap}>0
,
\gamma_{\rm elliptic}
,
b_{\rm elliptic}>0
of (
3.28
) and (
3.31
)
are fixed by the statement of Theorem
3.3

(see already Section
A.4.4
).

All
r
-parameters from Table
1
,
as well as the current parameters
e_{\rm red}
,
E
,
e_{\rm total}
,
the frequency range parameters
b_{\rm trap}
,
\gamma_{\rm ellitpic}
,
b_{\rm elliptic}

and the functions appearing in definitions (
3.50
)
and (
3.51
) (whose discussion is of course
deferred to Appendix
A
),
can now be viewed as chosen depending only on
a
and
M
.
In particular, when applied in Sections
4
–
6
, all the estimates of the present section have generic constants
C
which can be considered to
depend only on
a
,
M
(and
k
and
\delta
where appropriate) and possibly on
\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
(and an additional constant
\epsilon_{\rm redder}
to be introduced, which both still must be chosen in the context of Proposition
4.5.1
). Note again, however, the special
convention of
Section
2.1.5
regarding the dependence of generic constants
c
and
C
on

\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
,
\epsilon_{\rm redder}
(in particular when occurring in the product expressions
C\epsilon_{\rm cutoff}
). (We will thus have to continue to track the dependence of those constants explicitly in order
to eventually absorb such terms.)

4
Quasilinear equations: preliminaries and applications of the estimates and currents

We now at last may turn to our study of the quasilinear equation (
1.1
).

We first review in Section
4.1
the general class of nonlinearities
considered from
[
DHRT22
]
.
In Section
4.2
, we will review local well posedness for (
1.1
)
including useful Cauchy-type stability
statements.
We then introduce in Section
4.3
the basic smallness assumption
on
\psi
,
the extension
\widehat{\psi}
and
the additional top order energies which will play a central role in our argument.
In Section
4.4
,
we will promote the currents of Section
3.4
and
3.5

to covariant currents with respect to
g(\psi,x)
and infer stability properties of the coercivity statements,
up to suitable error terms. As outlined in Section
1.2.4
, we then apply in Section
4.5
these currents
at top order to (
1.1
), to obtain a basic energy estimate with various
error terms on the right hand side.
We estimate all terms on the right hand side in Section
4.6
in turn from
our energies, to obtain finally
a closed system of estimates in Section
4.7
.

4.1
The class of equations from
[
DHRT22
]

The class of equations considered will be exactly
as in Section 4.1 and 4.7 of
[
DHRT22
]
, specialised
to the case where
g_{(0,x)}=g_{a,M}
with
|a|<M
.
(We in particular again allow for now also the case
a=0
.)

We review here:
We will consider quasilinear equations of the form below:

\Box_{g(\psi,x)}\psi=N^{\mu\nu}(\psi,x)\partial_{\mu}\psi\,\partial_{\nu}\psi,

(4.1)

where we recall

g:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}\to T^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes T^{*}\mathcal{M},\qquad N:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}\to T\mathcal{M}\otimes T\mathcal{M}

(4.2)

satisfy
\pi\circ g(\psi,x)=x
,
\pi\circ N(\psi,x)=x

with
\pi:T\mathcal{M}\otimes T\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
,
\pi:T^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes T^{*}\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}

the canonical projections, and
such that

g(0,x)=g_{a,M}(x)

for all
x
while
g(\cdot,x)=g_{a,M}
for
r(x)\geq R/2
, and
N
and
g
are smooth, and, for each
k
,

\partial_{{\bf x}}^{{\beta}}\partial_{\xi}^{s}g^{\mu\nu}(\xi,x)
and

\partial_{{\bf x}}^{{\beta}}\partial_{\xi}^{s}N^{\mu\nu}(\xi,x)
are
uniformly bounded for all
|\beta|+s\leq k
, all
r\leq R
and
|\xi|\leq 1
, where

\partial_{{\bf x}}^{\beta}
is expressed in multi-index notation
with respect to the ambient Cartesian coordinates
recalled in Section
2.1
.

Our additional assumption on
N
will only depend on the region
r\geq 8R/9
, so we will need some notation to denote the restriction of energies, etc., to this region.
Given
r_{*}\geq r_{0}
and
v
, and a
\psi
defined on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, by

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(p)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}_{r_{*},v}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],\quad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}_{r_{*},v}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi],\quad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0+)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$k$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}_{r_{*},v}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]

we shall mean the expressions defined as in Section
2.3.3
, but where all domains of integration are restricted to the region
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)\cap\{r\geq r_{*}\}
.

With this, we may now recall our additional assumption on
N
capturing null structure (Assumption 4.7.1 of
[
DHRT22
]
):

Assumption 4.1.1

(Null structural assumption for semilinear terms
[
DHRT22
]
)
.

There exists a
k_{\rm null}
, and for all
k\geq k_{\rm null}
,
an
\varepsilon_{\rm null}>0
, such that the following holds for all
\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}
,
v
.

Let
\psi
be a smooth function defined on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)\cap\{r\geq 8R/9\}

satisfying the bound

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny$(0)$}}}{\underaccent{\scalebox{.8}{\mbox{\tiny$\ll\mkern-6.0muk$}}}{\mathcal{X}}}_{8R/9,v}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})[\psi]\leq\varepsilon

(4.3)

for
some
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{null}
.
Then for all
\delta\leq p\leq 2-\delta
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1},v)\cap\{r\geq R\}}
